A Scalable Distributed Quantum Optimization Framework via Factor Graph Paradigm
Yuwen Huang, Xiaojun Lin, Bin Luo, John C.S. Lui

Abstract
Distributed quantum computing (DQC) connects many small quantum processors into a single logical machine, offering a practical route to scalable quantum computation. However, most existing DQC paradigms are structure-agnostic. Circuit cutting proposed by Peng et al. in [Phys. Rev. Lett., Oct. 2020] reduces per-device qubits at the cost of exponential classical post-processing, while search-space partitioning proposed by Avron et al. in [Phys. Rev. A., Nov. 2021] distributes the workload but weakens Grover’s ideal quadratic speedup. In this paper, we introduce a structure-aware framework for distributed quantum optimization that resolves this complexity-resource trade-off. We model the objective function as a factor graph and expose its sparse interaction structure. We cut the graph along its natural “seams”, i.e. , a separator of boundary variables, to obtain loosely coupled subproblems that fit on resource-constrained processors. We coordinate these subproblems with shared entanglement, so the network executes a single globally coherent search rather than independent local searches. We prove that this design preserves Grover-like scaling: for a search space of size N N , our framework achieves O ⁡ ( N ) O(\sqrt{N}) query complexity up to processors and separator dependent factors, while relaxing the qubit requirement of each processor. We extend the framework with a hierarchical divide-and-conquer strategy that scales to large-scale optimization problems and supports two operating modes: a fully coherent mode for fault-tolerant networks and a hybrid mode that inserts measurements to cap circuit depth on near-term devices. We validate the predicted query-entanglement trade-offs through simulations over diverse network topologies, and we show that structure-aware decomposition delivers a practical path to scalable distributed quantum optimization on quantum networks.



a]Department of Computer Science and Engineering, The Chinese University of Hong Kong, Hong Kong SAR, China
b]Department of Information Engineering, The Chinese University of Hong Kong, Hong Kong SAR, China

1
Introduction

Quantum computing offers a potential to solve certain problems that are widely believed to be intractable for even the most powerful classical computers. Present quantum hardware, however, remains constrained by fundamental physical limits. Current devices operate in the noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) regime
[
33
]
, where processors of 50–100s of qubits are available but are susceptible to environmental noise and decoherence
[
16
,
12
]
. These imperfections restrict the achievable circuit depth and make the construction of a single, large-scale, monolithic fault-tolerant quantum computer a formidable long-term engineering challenge
[
17
]
.

An alternative approach to scalable quantum computation is a distributed, modular architecture that connects many smaller, higher-fidelity quantum processing units (QPUs)
[
28
,
36
]
. This modular approach, analogous to classical high-performance computing
[
15
]
, envisions a network of QPUs collaborating on a single computational task
[
18
]
. This distributed quantum computing (DQC) model uses quantum networks to establish shared entanglement between modules, enabling non-local operations and allowing the network to function as a single, coherent logical processor
[
32
]
. The central challenge, however, lies in designing quantum algorithms and network protocols that do not sacrifice the very quantum advantage they are meant to enable.

The current DQC literature is largely shaped by two
structure-agnostic
approaches that split a computation either at
the circuit level or at the input-space level.

Circuit cutting
decomposes a monolithic circuit into fragments that fit on separate QPUs and then reconstructs the
global outcome by classical post-processing of fragment statistics
[
30
,
34
,
31
]
. A common
instance is to replace nonlocal gates by sampling local operations (“virtual gates”)
[
27
]
. Circuit
cutting saves qubits but shifts the burden to classical computation: the reconstruction cost grows exponentially with the
number of inter-processor cuts
K
, typically as
O(4^{K})

[
30
,
31
]
or
O(9^{K})

[
27
]
.
When
K
is large, this overhead can erase the benefit of quantum speedups.

Search-space partitioning
instead divides a search space of size
N
across
K
processors, which run independent
local searches and coordinate only through classical communication
[
2
]
. This breaks the coherence required
by Grover’s algorithm: rather than the
O(\sqrt{N})
queries of a monolithic search, the total query count becomes

O(K\sqrt{N/K})=O(\sqrt{KN})

[
2
]
. The extra factor
\sqrt{K}
implies that adding processors to
satisfy local qubit limits makes the computation harder in
K
.
These limitations share a common cause: both paradigms distribute the workload without maintaining global quantum
coherence across processors, which is essential for quadratic speedups.
This motivates a
structure-aware
alternative: decompose the problem along a small set of boundary variables so that
most computation can be localized, while a limited amount of shared entanglement preserves coherence across the boundary
and enables Grover-like global amplification.
Other decomposition methods, such as entanglement forging
[
13
]
, target specific settings (
e.g.
, a bipartite
cut with low entanglement) and therefore do not provide a general framework for distributed optimization.

This paper introduces a
structure-aware
paradigm for distributed quantum optimization that resolves the complexity–resource trade-off by using
two resources that structure-agnostic methods leave on the table:
problem structure
and
global coherence
.
We represent the objective function with a factor graph
[
20
]
, which makes sparse interactions explicit through a
sum of local terms. We then cut the graph along a small set of
boundary variables

\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}
and assign each
resulting subproblem to a resource-constrained worker QPU. The key technical point is that the workers do not run as
independent solvers: shared entanglement lets the network implement the required distributed primitives while keeping
the computation coherent across subproblems.

Table 1:
Quantitative comparison of DQC paradigms.
Here
N=2^{n}
and
K
denotes the number of workers (or the number of cuts in circuit cutting).
Query complexity counts oracle queries. “Light” post-processing means polynomial-time aggregation or coordination.

Work

Classical post-processing

Inter-node communication

This work

O(\sqrt{N})

Light (poly-time aggregation)

Shared EPR pairs and classical messages (to maintain global coherence)

Peng
et al.

[
30
]

O(\sqrt{N})

O\bigl(4^{K}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(n)\bigr)

Classical messages (fragment outcomes for reconstruction)

Avron
et al.

[
2
]

O(\sqrt{KN})

Light (poly-time coordination)

Classical messages (coordination only)

This design yields a concrete win. For a search space of size
N
, our distributed algorithm preserves Grover-like scaling:
it uses
O(\sqrt{N})
oracle queries, up to factors that depend on the number of processors and the chosen separator,
while reducing the qubit footprint of each worker to
O\bigl(|\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}|\bigr)
for a local search space of size

2^{|\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}|}
. Table
1
makes this advantage explicit by contrasting our trade-offs with circuit cutting and search-space partitioning. We further corroborate the predicted resource drivers with simulations on
quantum networks of diverse topologies.

In short, we pay a small boundary search and shared entanglement to cut per-QPU qubits sharply, while retaining Grover-like scaling.

Main Contributions.

•

Structure-aware DQC via factor graphs:

We propose a distributed framework that decomposes a broad class of structured optimization problems using a factor
graph separator
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}
, producing subproblems that fit on small QPUs while remaining coupled through a coherent
network protocol.

•

Grover-like query scaling with reduced per-QPU qubits:

We show that the resulting distributed algorithm achieves
O(\sqrt{N})
query complexity up to processor and
separator dependent factors, while each worker uses only
O\bigl(|\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}|\bigr)
qubits. We corroborate these claims with gate-level state vector simulations on small instances, which provide end-to-end sanity checks and confirm the predicted scaling trends.

•

Hierarchical divide-and-conquer for arbitrary size:

We extend the single-level framework by a recursive construction that handles oversized subproblems. We define
(i) a fully coherent mode, suited to fault-tolerant networks, and (ii) a hybrid mode that inserts measurements to cap
circuit depth on NISQ devices, and we quantify the resulting query–entanglement trade-off in simulation.

•

End-to-end protocol and resource guarantees:

We specify the communication protocol and derive performance guarantees for success probability, query complexity,
and quantum-network resources, including per-QPU qubits and EPR-pair consumption.

The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces the network model,
factor-graph formulation, and decomposition strategy. Section
3
presents the
distributed algorithm and its guarantees. Section
4
describes the distributed
implementation of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
, and Section
5
analyzes overall performance and
resource use. Section
6
extends the framework with a scalable hierarchical design for both
near-term and fault-tolerant hardware. Section
7
concludes the paper.
Appendix
A
lists basic notation. The remaining appendices provide proofs and additional results, including simulation studies (Appendices
M
and
P
).

2
Foundations: Quantum Network, Optimization Framework, and Decomposition

This section establishes the foundational concepts for the distributed quantum algorithm presented in Section
3
. We first define the quantum network model, then define the factor graph optimization framework, and finally present a problem decomposition strategy. These elements are crucial for structuring and distributing complex optimization tasks across multiple quantum processors.

\captionsetup

font=scriptsize

\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
\mathrm{QPU}_{1}
\mathrm{QPU}_{2}
\mathrm{QPU}_{3}

Figure 1:
An illustration of an example quantum network, where
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
is the coordinator processor and
\mathrm{QPU}_{1},\mathrm{QPU}_{2},\mathrm{QPU}_{3}
are worker processors.

2.1
The Quantum Network Model

A quantum network comprises one coordinator quantum processor (
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
) and
N_{\mathrm{w}}
worker quantum processors (
\mathrm{QPU}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{QPU}_{N_{\mathrm{w}}}
). Network operations are restricted to local operations and classical communications (LOCCs) (see,
e.g.
,
[
11
]
): each processor executes quantum operations only on its local qubits. Processors communicate classically and can share Einstein-Podolsky-Rosen (EPR) pairs with their direct neighbors.

We represent the network as a graph
\mathsf{N}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{N}},\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{N}})
. (Fig.
1
illustrates a simple example where
N_{\mathsf{G}}=3
.)

1.

The vertex set
\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{N}}=\{\mathrm{c}\}\cup\{n_{\mathrm{w}}\mid n_{\mathrm{w}}\in[N_{\mathrm{w}}]\}
corresponds to the quantum processors, with
\mathrm{c}
denoting
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
and
n_{\mathrm{w}}
denoting
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathrm{w}}}
.

2.

An edge
(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{N}}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{N}}
indicates that processors
\mathrm{QPU}_{v_{1}}
and
\mathrm{QPU}_{v_{2}}
are direct neighbors, enabling them to establish shared EPR pairs and a classical communication channel.

We assume
\mathsf{N}
is a connected graph. This connectivity ensures two properties:

1.

Any two processors can communicate classically, possibly via intermediate nodes.

2.

Shared EPR pairs can be established between
\mathrm{QPU}_{v_{1}}
and
\mathrm{QPU}_{v_{2}}
either directly if
(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{N}}
, or indirectly using entanglement swapping
[
37
]
along a path in
\mathsf{N}
.

Throughout this paper, we assume that any pair of processors in the network can share EPR pairs and communicate classically, thereby enabling operations such as quantum teleportation
[
29
, Chapter 1.3.7]
.

2.2
Factor Graph-Based Optimization Framework

Factor graphs can be used for representing the factorization of multivariate functions. Such representation is instrumental in defining a general optimization framework applicable to diverse problems. In this paper, factor graphs serve to visualize problem structure and to guide a decomposition strategy (Section
2.3
), which is essential for our proposed distributed quantum algorithm (Section
3
).

Definition 1

(Factor graph-based optimization problem)
.

A factor graph
\mathsf{G}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{X})
consists of:

1.

A bipartite graph
(\mathcal{F}\cup\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
, where
\mathcal{F}
is the set of function nodes and
\mathcal{V}
is the set of variable nodes. Each edge
(v,f)\in\mathcal{E}
connects a variable node
v\in\mathcal{V}
to a function node
f\in\mathcal{F}
.

2.

An alphabet
\mathcal{X}\triangleq\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{X}_{v}
, where
\mathcal{X}_{v}=[N_{v}^{\mathrm{card}}]
is the finite alphabet for variable
x_{v}
of cardinality
N_{v}^{\mathrm{card}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.

Based on
\mathsf{G}
, we make the following definitions.

3.

We define a configuration
\mathbf{x}\triangleq(x_{v})_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\in\mathcal{X}
to be an assignment of values to all variable nodes.

4.

For any
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
, we define
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\triangleq\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}_{v}
and
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\triangleq(x_{v})_{v\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}
to be the alphabet and a partial configuration for variables in
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.

5.

For each function node
f\in\mathcal{F}
, we define the set
\partial f\subseteq\mathcal{V}
to be the set of variable nodes adjacent to
f
.

6.

Each function node
f\in\mathcal{F}
corresponds to a local function
f:\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}\to\mathbb{R}
that maps configurations of its neighboring variables
\mathbf{x}_{\partial f}
to a value
f(\mathbf{x}_{\partial f})\in\mathbb{R}
.

7.

We define the global function
g:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
to be
g(\mathbf{x})\triangleq\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}}f(\mathbf{x}_{\partial f})
.

8.

We define
\mathbf{x}_{\max}
via an optimization problem: finding the
\mathbf{x}
that maximize
g(\mathbf{x})
, i.e., finding

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{\max}\triangleq\arg\max\limits_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\,g(\mathbf{x}).

(1)

and the associated maximum value is

\displaystyle g_{\max}\triangleq\max\limits_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\,g(\mathbf{x}).

(2)

\triangle

Factor graphs have wide applications
[
20
,
22
,
35
]
, including theoretical computer science (
e.g.
, satisfiability (SAT) problems), coding theory (
e.g.
, decoding algorithms for low-density parity-check (LDPC) or turbo codes), and portfolio optimization.
Appendix
B
presents a factor-graph formulation of a local portfolio investment problem. The formulation is readily generalized to a broader class of local portfolio investment problems, including NP-complete variants considered in
[
24
]
.
One can use factor graphs to represent the complicated dependencies among local functions with a structured graphical form, which is particularly useful for the case where the global function naturally factorizes over local interactions.

Note that the factor graph defined above is based on the semigroup
(\mathbb{R},+)
. We emphasis that
the main contribution in this paper,
i.e.
, our proposed distributed quantum algorithms, can be adapted for the factor graphs defined based on one of the semigroups in
(\mathbb{R},\max)
,
(\mathbb{R},\min)
, and
(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\times)
, by appropriately modifying how values are combined or compared within the algorithmic steps.

It is sufficient to consider binary variables and normalized global function
.
For compatibility with quantum algorithm design, particularly for encoding values into quantum states in qubits, in the remaining part of this paper, we consider factor graphs with
binary variables
(
x_{v}\in\{0,1\}
for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
),
nonnegative-valued
local functions,
i.e.
,
f(\mathbf{x}_{\partial f})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mathbf{x}_{\partial f}\in\mathcal{X}_{\partial f}
, and a global function
g(\mathbf{x})
normalized to the
interval

[0,1]
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
.
Appendix
C
(Proposition
19
) details a transformation that converts any factor graph under Definition
1
into an equivalent factor graph possessing these binary, nonnegativity, and normalized properties. This transformation preserves the essential structure of the optimization problem, allowing the solution
(g_{\max},\mathbf{x}_{\max})
of the original factor graph to be recovered from the solution of the transformed factor graph.

2.3
Decomposition for Distributed Execution

Our goal is to break a large factor-graph optimization problem into subproblems that fit on QPUs with limited qubits and limited connectivity. We encode each discrete binary variable into one qubit; for large graphs, this quickly exceeds the capacity of any single processor. In order to make the problem distributable, we choose a small set of boundary variables and condition on them so that the remaining graph splits into smaller components. Each component can then be solved on a different QPU, in parallel, with coordination only through the boundary.

Towards this end, our key observation is that the factor graphs of many applications are
local
: each factor involves only nearby variables (
e.g.
, nearest-neighbor Markov random fields and sparse parity-check codes
[
35
]
). Under such locality, small vertex separators typically disconnect large regions, so a modest boundary set
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}
suffices to yield balanced subgraphs that can be mapped to distinct QPUs with limited qubits and connectivity. See, for example, standard treatments of factor graphs and localized interactions
[
20
,
35
]
. This section formalizes that split and links it directly to the distributed algorithm in Section
3
.

Definition 2

(Boundary-based split of a factor graph)
.

Let
\mathsf{G}=(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{X})
be a factor graph (Definition
1
).
We fix a boundary set
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
of
variable
nodes and form the reduced bipartite graph

\mathsf{G}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}\triangleq\bigl(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}},\{(f,v)\in\mathcal{E}:\ v\notin\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}\}\bigr),

i.e., delete all boundary variables in
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}
and their incident edges. Let

\mathsf{G}_{\mathrm{s},1},\ldots,\mathsf{G}_{\mathrm{s},N_{\mathsf{G}}}

denote the connected components of
\mathsf{G}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}
that contain at least one variable node.
For each
n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]
, we write
\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}\subseteq\mathcal{F}

for the variable-node set and function-node set in
\mathsf{G}_{\mathrm{s},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
, respectively.
By construction, the factor sets
\{\mathcal{F}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}\}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
are disjoint; hence each factor (function) node
belongs to exactly one subgraph
\mathsf{G}_{\mathrm{s},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
.
Finally, we define the boundary neighborhood of
\mathsf{G}_{\mathrm{s},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\,v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}\ \middle|\ \exists f\in\mathcal{F}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}\ \text{s.t.}\ (f,v)\in\mathcal{E}\,\right\}=\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}\cap\bigcup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}}\partial f,

i.e., the boundary variables adjacent to at least one factor in
\mathcal{F}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
.

For each boundary assignment
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}
and each
n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]
, we define the
local conditioned objective
based on its internal variables and the boundary variables to be

\displaystyle g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}})\triangleq\sum_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}}f(\mathbf{x}_{\partial f}),\qquad\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}}\in\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}}\mathcal{X}_{v},

and define its maximum to be

g^{\max}_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}\triangleq\max\limits_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}})
.
The overall optimization objective, defined as a function of the
boundary variables
, is then

\displaystyle g_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}})\triangleq\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}g^{\max}_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}.

Maximizing
g
reduces to a search over the boundary:

\displaystyle g_{\max}=\max_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}}g_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}).

\triangle

See Fig.
7
in Appendix
B
for an illustration of the decomposition in Definition
2
.

Choosing the boundary.

In practice, there are several reasonable strategies for selecting
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}
.
A useful guiding principle is to choose a
small
boundary that separates the factor graph into subgraphs of comparable size. This choice balances the computational load across subproblems while keeping the boundary-induced overhead small.
One principled method uses a junction-tree representation: for two adjacent cliques, the separator is given by the intersection of their variable sets, and a small intersection yields a compact separator
[
21
]
.
Accordingly, the example in Fig.
7
in Appendix
B
adopts the boundary suggested by the corresponding junction-tree separator.

3
A Distributed Quantum Algorithm for Solving the Factor Graph-based Optimization Problem

This section presents
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
, a distributed quantum algorithm to solve the general factor graph-based optimization problem from expression (
2
).
The algorithm operates on the quantum network (Section
2.1
) and leverages the problem decomposition (Definition
2
).
This alignment of the computation with the problem’s topology enables a scalable approach to distributed quantum optimization.

The algorithm performs a quantum-accelerated binary search to find the global maximum,
g_{\max}
.
The core of this search is a quantum phase estimation subroutine that serves as a comparison mechanism.
This global search, coordinated by the
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
, is built upon a nested architecture: each query coherently executes local quantum optimization routines in parallel on all worker processors. This coherent parallelism is enabled by a crucial state preparation unitary,
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
.
The complex distributed protocol for constructing this operator is a cornerstone of our framework and is detailed in Section
4
.

For clarity, a comprehensive summary of all quantum registers is provided in Appendix
D
, the distributed implementations of core unitary components are detailed in Appendix
F
, and the rigorous proof of this section’s main theorem is presented in Appendix
G
.

Assumption 3
.

In Sections
3
–
5
, we suppose that the number of worker QPUs is equal to the number of sub-graphs, i.e.,
N_{\mathrm{w}}=N_{\mathsf{G}}
.

\triangle

3.1
The Core Unitary: An Amplitude Amplification Operator

The iterative binary search is driven by a family of amplitude amplification operators.
Each operator performs a Grover-type rotation that increases the amplitude of states
whose encoded objective value exceeds a prescribed threshold.

Registers induced by the decomposition.

Recall that removing the boundary-variable set
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}
decomposes the factor graph into

N_{\mathsf{G}}
disjoint subgraphs
\{\mathsf{G}_{\mathrm{s},n_{\mathsf{G}}}\}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
.
We allocate three groups of qubits:

•

\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}}
: the
boundary register
, which stores an encoding of a boundary configuration
(
i.e.
, an assignment to the boundary variables in
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}
).

•

\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{loc},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
: the collection of
local registers
associated with the
N_{\mathsf{G}}
partitions.
In particular,
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{loc},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
contains the per-partition
value registers

(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
, where each
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}}\in(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes N_{\mathrm{p}}}

stores an
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit encoding of the local optimum value for partition
n_{\mathsf{G}}
.

•

\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{aux}}
: auxiliary / work registers used by state preparation and by the marking circuitry.
We single out
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{aux,c}}\subseteq\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{aux}}
for ancillas that are required to be returned to
\ket{\mathbf 0}
.

The exact qubit-level layouts are given in Appendix
D
; for the discussion below, it suffices to know
which information is stored in
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}}
and in the value registers
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
. We next introduce two important unitary operators that form the basis of our algorithm.

A key state-preparation unitary.

Given a boundary configuration, each partition
\mathsf{G}_{\mathrm{s},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
faces a local sub-problem.
The optimal value of this local sub-problem generally depends on the boundary configuration.
We therefore introduce a crucial unitary
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
that prepares,
coherently
,
the local optima for
all
partitions and across the superposition of
all
boundary configurations.
Formally,
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
acts on
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{loc},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{aux}})
and satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}\cdot\ket{\mathbf 0}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}}}\ket{\mathbf 0}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{loc},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}}\ket{\mathbf 0}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{aux}}}=\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{ini}}}\ket{\mathbf 0}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{aux,c}}}.

(3)

We assume that the prepared state
\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{ini}}}
on the RHS of (
3
) satisfies the following two properties.
Let
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}^{\ast}
be an optimal boundary assignment attaining
g_{\max}
, and let

\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\triangleq(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}

denote the corresponding tuple of
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit encodings of the
local
optimal objective values
\bigl(g^{\max}_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}^{\ast}}\bigr)_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
under
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}^{\ast}
.
1
1

1

Our distributed quantum algorithm aims to recover both the global optimum value
g_{\max}
and an optimizer
\mathbf{x}_{\max}
.
For this reason, we track
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
, which compactly represents the local optima induced by the optimal boundary assignment
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}^{\ast}
.
During the algorithm, for each candidate boundary assignment
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}
, the local optimal values are coherently encoded into an entangled
register state. The algorithm then uses a quantum phase-estimation routine to extract the values corresponding to
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}^{\ast}
.

1.

Sufficient weight on the optimal outcome.

Define
p_{\min,\mathrm{c}}\triangleq(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}N_{\mathsf{G}}}/|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}|
.
The state
\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}
assigns a non-negligible probability
p_{\min,\mathrm{c}}
to the basis outcome where the value registers
equal
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
:

\displaystyle\bra{\psi_{\rmini}}\bigl(\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{rem}}\otimes\mathbf{P}_{\{\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\}}\bigr)\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}\geq p_{\min,\mathrm{c}}\geq 0,

(4)

where the projection operator
\mathbf{P}_{\{\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\}}
, as defined in Definition
16
in Appendix
A
, projects to the subspace consisting of the branch associated with
\ket{\mathbf{z}_{\max,\rmp,[\Nsfn]}}_{(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}}
.

2.

Accuracy of the encoded objective value.

The decoded sum of these
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit values is a tight lower approximation of
g_{\max}
:

\displaystyle 0\leq g_{\max}-\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}z_{\mathrm{dec}}\bigl(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}}\bigr)\leq N_{\mathsf{G}}\cdot 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}},

(5)

where for an
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit string
\mathbf{z}=\bigl(z(1),\ldots,z(N_{\mathrm{p}})\bigr)\in\{0,1\}^{N_{\mathrm{p}}}
, the threshold encoding
z_{\mathrm{dec}}
is defined to be

\displaystyle z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z})\triangleq\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{p}}}z(k)\cdot 2^{-k}\in[0,1).

(6)

(See Appendix
A
for further discussion and related operators.)

Section
4
constructs a state-preparation unitary
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
that
satisfies these requirements. In this section, we treat
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
as a black box and assume only the
specification in (
3
)–(
5
).

Amplitude amplification operator.

We define the set of local-value strings whose
sum
exceeds the threshold
\mathbf{z}
to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}\triangleq\left\{\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\in\!\!\!\prod_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\!\!\!\{0,1\}^{N_{\mathrm{p}}}\ \middle|\ \sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\!\!\!z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})\geq z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z})\right\}.

(7)

Then, we define the following amplitude amplification operator, which generalizes the idea behind Grover’s search algorithm
[
14
]
.

Definition 4

(
Amplitude amplification operator

[
7
]
)
.

For each threshold string
\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{N_{\mathrm{p}}}
, we define

\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}}
on the registers
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{loc},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{aux}})
to be

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}}\triangleq\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}\cdot\bigl(2\ket{\mathbf 0}\bra{\mathbf 0}-\mathbf{I}\bigr)_{\mathrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}^{\mathsf{H}}\cdot\bigl(\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{rem}}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}}\bigr).

(8)

where

•

\bigl(2\ket{\mathbf 0}\bra{\mathbf 0}-\mathbf{I}\bigr)_{\mathrm{all}}
is the reflection about the
all-zero computational basis state on the full workspace
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{loc},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{aux}})
,

•

\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}}
is a phase oracle that flips the sign of computational-basis states
whose local value registers satisfy (
7
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}}\ket{\mathbf z_{\mathrm p,[\Nsfn]}}\triangleq\begin{cases}-\ket{\mathbf z_{\mathrm p,[\Nsfn]}}&\text{if }\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}\\
\phantom{-}\ket{\mathbf z_{\mathrm p,[\Nsfn]}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\quad,

and act on the value registers
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
(and any required ancillas),

•

\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{rem}}
denotes the identity on the remaining registers.

\triangle

Implementation details for the reflection and oracle are provided in
Section
4
and Appendix
F
.

3.2
The Distributed Algorithm: Procedure and Formalism

Algorithm 1

Distributed quantum algorithm for factor graph optimization (bit-by-bit refinement)

1:

Input:
Factor graph
\mathsf{G}
, network
\mathsf{N}
, error parameter
\delta\in(0,1)
, precision bits
N_{\mathrm{p}}
.

2:

Output:
A measured string
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}\in\{0,1\}^{N_{\mathrm{p}}}
in
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}
that lower-approximates
g_{\max}
.

3:

Initialization:
Initialize all quantum registers across the network to
\ket{\mathbf 0}
and set
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}=\ket{\mathbf 0}
.

4:

for

n_{\mathrm{p}}=1,\ldots,N_{\mathrm{p}}

do

5:

Form candidate threshold:
interpret each computational basis state associated with
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}(1:n_{\mathrm{p}}-1)
as
\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}}
and set

\mathbf{z}^{(n_{\mathrm{p}})}\triangleq(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1,0,\ldots,0)
.

6:

Threshold test (writes
q_{\mathrm{p,c}}(n_{\mathrm{p}})
):
for each computational basis state associated with
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}(1:n_{\mathrm{p}}-1)
, coherently implement

\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe-sub}}\left(\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}},\,\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}^{(n_{\mathrm{p}})}},p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\,\delta\right)
in a distributed manner
so that the decision bit is written to
q_{\mathrm{p,c}}(n_{\mathrm{p}})
and all other registers are (approximately) restored.

7:

end

for

8:

Measure and output:
Measure
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}
in the computational basis to obtain
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}
.

Algorithm
1
is the main algorithmic contribution of this paper.
It outputs an
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit string
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}
that lower-approximates the global optimum
g_{\max}
.
The algorithm proceeds
by increasing precision
: it determines the bits of
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}
from the most
significant bit to the least significant bit, refining the approximation by one bit per iteration.

We first outline the high-level flow of the algorithm and its use of the amplitude-amplification operator defined earlier. The coordinator maintains the
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-qubit register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}
, which stores the running
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit estimate of
g_{\max}
.
At iteration
n_{\mathrm{p}}\in[N_{\mathrm{p}}]
, we assume that the first
n_{\mathrm{p}}-1
qubits of
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}
encode a prefix

\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}}\in\{0,1\}^{n_{\mathrm{p}}-1}
(possibly in superposition).
In order to determine the next bit, the coordinator performs a threshold test against the candidate value

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}^{(n_{\mathrm{p}})}\triangleq\bigl(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},\,1,\,0,\ldots,0\bigr)\in\{0,1\}^{N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

If
g_{\max}\geq z_{\mathrm{dec}}\bigl(\mathbf{z}^{(n_{\mathrm{p}})}\bigr)
, it sets the state associated with
q_{\mathrm{p,c}}(n_{\mathrm{p}})
to be
\ket{1}
; otherwise it sets
\ket{0}
.
Repeating this procedure for
n_{\mathrm{p}}=1,\ldots,N_{\mathrm{p}}
yields an
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit approximation of
g_{\max}
.
This threshold test is implemented coherently by invoking the
amplitude-amplification operator
[
8
]

\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}^{(n_{\mathrm{p}})}}
, which marks states that exceeds the threshold via a phase flip and amplifies their support.

The comparison “
g_{\max}\geq z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z})
?” is implemented via the amplitude-amplification operator

\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}}
(Definition
4
).
Recall that
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}}
is built from
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
and a marking oracle

\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}}
that phase-flips precisely those computational-basis states whose encoded local
values exceeds the threshold
\mathbf{z}
. The key point is that
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}}
does not need to know
g_{\max}
; instead, the inequality
g_{\max}\geq z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z})
can be determined by checking whether the marked subspace has nonzero overlap with
\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}
.
Intuitively:

•

if
g_{\max}<z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z})
, then no assignment satisfies the threshold, so the oracle marks nothing;

•

if
g_{\max}\geq z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z})
, then the marked subspace has nonzero weight under the prepared state,
and
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}}
induces a nontrivial Grover-type rotation.

Thus, deciding whether
g_{\max}\geq z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z})
reduces to testing whether the relevant eigenphase of

\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}}
is zero or nonzero.

In order to implement the above threshold test coherently within Algorithm
1
, we invoke an approximately reversible
controlled phase-test
unitary,
denoted by

\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe-sub}}\left(\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}},\,\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}},p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta\right).

It acts on the joint registers

\bigl(\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c},n_{\mathrm{p}}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{loc},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{aux}},q_{\mathrm{p,c}}(n_{\mathrm{p}})\bigr)

and serves as a black-box decision primitive:
conditioned on the current candidate threshold
\mathbf{z}
, it writes a single decision bit to the designated output qubit
(here,
q_{\mathrm{p,c}}(n_{\mathrm{p}})
), while uncomputing its workspace so that all other registers are (approximately) returned to their
pre-call states. We defer the circuit-level construction and error analysis of this primitive to
Appendix
E
and also a distributed implementation to Appendix
F
; the details are not needed to follow the main control flow in
Algorithm
1
.

Note that the implementation of the
n_{\mathrm{p}}
-th iteration of Algorithm
1
can be equivalently written as a block-diagonal controlled unitary
with respect to the already-determined prefix register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}(1:n_{\mathrm{p}}-1)
.
For each
n_{\mathrm{p}}\in[N_{\mathrm{p}}]
, the network applies

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}}\in\{0,1\}^{n_{\mathrm{p}}-1}}

\displaystyle\biggl(\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe-sub}}\left(\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}},\,\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}^{(n_{\mathrm{p}})}},p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\,\delta\right)\biggr)\otimes\ket{\mathbf z_{\mathrm{prefix}}}\bra{\mathbf z_{\mathrm{prefix}}}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}(1:n_{\mathrm{p}}-1)}.

(9)

where
\mathbf{z}^{(n_{\mathrm{p}})}
is the candidate threshold used at iteration
n_{\mathrm{p}}
.
The phase-test primitive writes its binary outcome to
q_{\mathrm{p,c}}(n_{\mathrm{p}})
, thereby extending the prefix by one bit.
After
N_{\mathrm{p}}
iterations, measuring
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}
yields the final estimate
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}
.

3.3
Performance Guarantees

The performance guarantees for Algorithm
1
depend on the properties of the state
\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}=\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}\ket{\mathbf{0}}
,
i.e.
, (
4
) and (
5
).

Theorem 5

(
Algorithm Performance Guarantee
)
.

Under the conditions in (
4
) and (
5
), for a precision of
N_{\mathrm{p}}
bits and a failure parameter
\delta\in(0,1)
, Algorithm
1
has the following performance guarantees:

1.

Query complexity.
The total number of applications of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
or
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}^{\mathsf{H}}
is

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{queries}}

\displaystyle=N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot\Bigl(2^{\,t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+2}-2\Bigr)\in O\Bigl(N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot(1-\delta)^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}N_{\mathsf{G}}}\cdot\sqrt{|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}|}\Bigr),

where the qubit-counting function
t
for phase estimation is defined to be

\displaystyle t(c,\delta)\triangleq\left\lceil-(1/2)\cdot\log_{2}\left(\delta\cdot c\right)-1/2\right\rceil.

(10)

2.

Success probability and output.
With probability at least
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}
, a measurement of the coordinator’s result register,
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}
, yields the
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit string
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}
, which is the largest
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit value not exceeding the sum of the local maxima:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}\triangleq&\arg\max_{\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{c}}\in\{0,1\}^{N_{\mathrm{p}}}}\quad z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{c}})\\
&\hskip 34.14322pt\text{s.t.}\quad z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{c}})\leq\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}&z_{\mathrm{dec}}\bigl(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}}\bigr)\end{aligned}\quad.

(11)

Recall that
z_{\mathrm{dec}}
is defined in (
6
).
In order to ensure
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}
for any
0<\delta^{\prime}<1
, Bernoulli’s inequality implies that it suffices to choose

\delta\leq\delta^{\prime}/(2N_{\mathrm{p}}).

3.

Additive-error certificate.
The reported value
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}
is a certified lower bound on the true global maximum
g_{\max}
, satisfying:

\displaystyle 0\leq g_{\max}-z_{\mathrm{dec}}\bigl(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}\bigr)\leq(N_{\mathsf{G}}+1)\cdot 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

(12)

Proof.

See Appendix
G
.
∎

Theorem
5
thus characterizes both the complexity and the performance of Algorithm
1
.
Part
1
states the quantum advantage of the algorithm in terms of the query complexity.
Part
2
defines the output as the best
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit approximation of the
summed local solutions
, not the true global maximum.
Part
3
connects this output to the true global maximum
g_{\max}
by incorporating the local encoding error from (
5
).

4
Distributed Construction of the Unitary
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}

This section details the distributed implementation of the state preparation unitary
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
, a cornerstone of the main algorithm.
A primary challenge in distributed quantum computing is parallelizing a computation across multiple processors while maintaining the global quantum coherence required for a collective speedup.
The
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
operator is our solution to this challenge. It constructs the state
\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{initial}}}
(defined in (
3
)), which is a coherent superposition across all boundary configurations where each branch is entangled with the optimal solutions of the corresponding local sub-problems.
This procedure, a quantum analogue of classically solving all sub-problems for every possible boundary assignment, is performed in a single, coherent operation, thereby preserving the potential for a global quantum advantage.

Our presentation follows a top-down approach. First, we introduce the generic algorithmic building block for function maximization.
Second, we detail the distributed protocol that leverages this block to construct
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
.
Finally, we analyze the performance and resource costs of this construction.

4.1
A Generic Subroutine for Quantum Maximization

The local optimization at each worker QPU is a standard quantum task: finding the maximum value of a function.
Abstracting this task into a generic, reusable subroutine allows the analysis of local computational complexity to be separated from the complexities of distributed communication and coordination.

Consider the objective of finding the maximum value,
F^{\max}\triangleq\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{N}}F(\mathbf{y})
, of an arbitrary function
F:\{0,1\}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
.
A quantum algorithm for this task requires access to two types of operators.

1.

State Preparation Oracle
\mathbf{U}_{F}
:
This unitary operation prepares an initial state
\ket{\psi_{F,\mathrm{ini}}}
that has a non-zero overlap with the subspace of optimal solutions.
Specifically, we assume that

\displaystyle\bra{\psi_{F,\mathrm{ini}}}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Y}^{\max}}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{rem}})\ket{\psi_{F,\mathrm{ini}}}\geq p_{\min,F}>0,

(13)

where
\mathcal{Y}^{\max}\triangleq\arg\max\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{N}}F(\mathbf{y})
is the set of configurations that maximize
F
, the operator
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Y}^{\max}}
is the projector onto this set, and
p_{\min,F}
is a lower bound on the success probability.
As established in Appendix
H
, which details how classical randomized algorithms can be unitarily implemented, the existence of such an oracle is justified.

2.

Marking Unitary Operator
\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{F\geq\mathbf{z}}}
:
This unitary, formally defined in Appendix
I
, applies a phase of
-1
to any computational basis state
\ket{\mathbf{y}}
for which the function value
F(\mathbf{y})
is greater than or equal to a specified threshold
z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z})
.

Equipped with these oracles, a generic subroutine,
\mathbf{U}_{\max}(N_{\mathrm{p}},\delta,p_{\min,F},\mathbf{U}_{F})
, exists to find an
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit approximation of
F^{\max}
with high probability.
The detailed construction of this subroutine is provided in Appendix
I
.
The performance of this generic subroutine is summarized in the following proposition.

Proposition 6

(Performance of Generic Quantum Maximization)
.

For an arbitrary function
F:\{0,1\}^{N}\to[0,1]
, the generic quantum maximization algorithm

\mathbf{U}_{\max}(N_{\mathrm{p}},\delta,p_{\min,F},\mathbf{U}_{F})
has the following properties:

1.

Query Complexity:
The total number of queries to the oracles
\mathbf{U}_{F}
and its inverse is
N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot(2^{t(p_{\min,F},\delta)+2}-2)
, where the function
t
,
as defined in (
10
), determines the query precision.

2.

Success Probability and Outcome:
By measuring the qubits that store the approximation, the algorithm produces the best
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit lower approximation of
F^{\max}
, denoted
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},F}
, with a probability of at least
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.

3.

Approximation Precision:
The final approximation satisfies the bound
0\leq F^{\max}-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},F})\leq 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
I
.
∎

4.2
Distributed Protocol for Constructing
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}

The construction of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
is itself a distributed algorithm, built upon the generic quantum maximization subroutine from Proposition
6
.
This subroutine is instantiated at each worker processor with a set of local oracles, which we define first.

Definition 7

(Local Oracles)
.

For each subgraph
n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]
and each fixed configuration
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}
of its relevant boundary variables, we assume the corresponding worker processor
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
can implement a local state preparation oracle, denoted
\mathbf{U}_{g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}}
.
This unitary is a specific instance of the generic oracle
\mathbf{U}_{F}
from Proposition
6
, where the function
F
is the conditioned local objective
g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}
.
Acting on the local registers for internal variables (
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
) and oracle ancillas (
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
), both initialized to
\ket{\mathbf{0}}
, its action is to prepare an initial state:

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}}\cdot\left(\ket{\mathbf{0}}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}\otimes\ket{\mathbf{0}}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}\right)=\ket{ \psi_{\nsfn,\mathrm{ini}} }.

(14)

This prepared state must have a non-trivial overlap with the subspace of optimal solutions. We assume a minimum success probability
p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}}>0
such that for all
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}
:

\displaystyle\bra{ \psi_{\nsfn,\mathrm{ini}} }\bigl(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Y}_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}^{\max}}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}\bigr)\ket{ \psi_{\nsfn,\mathrm{ini}} }\geq p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}},

(15)

where
\mathcal{Y}_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}^{\max}\triangleq\arg\max\limits_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}})
is the set of optimal local configurations, and
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Y}_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}^{\max}}
is the projector onto that subspace (formally defined in Definition
16
, Appendix
A
).

\triangle

Algorithm 2

Distributed Protocol for Implementing
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}

1:

Stage 1: Distribute Boundary Information (at Coordinator
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
)

2:

Initialize a uniform superposition over all boundary configurations in register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}}
.

3:

for
each worker
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]

do

4:

Coherently fan out boundary bits by entangling
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}}
with the auxiliary register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
via CNOTs.

5:

Transmit the register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
to
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
via quantum teleportation.

6:

end

for

7:

Stage 2: Parallel Local Maximization (at all Workers
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
)

8:

In parallel, each worker executes the generic maximization subroutine (Proposition
6
) on its local function, conditioned on the state of its received register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
. The
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit result is stored in the local qubit register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
.

9:

Stage 3: Aggregate Quantum Results (at Coordinator
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
)

10:

for
each worker
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]

do

11:

Receive the registers
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})
from
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
via quantum teleportation.

12:

end

for

13:

Reverse the entanglement operations from Stage 1 to disentangle
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}}
and return the auxiliary registers
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
to the
\ket{\mathbf{0}}
state.

With the local oracles defined, we present the distributed protocol for implementing the unitary operation
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
in Algorithm
2
, which acts on the qubits specified in Appendix
D
.
The protocol’s core is the parallel local maximization (Stage 2), formally described by the application of the following controlled operation at each worker
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|}}

\displaystyle\ket{\mathbf{x}_{\setVbnsfn}}\bra{\mathbf{x}_{\setVbnsfn}}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{\max}\bigl(N_{\mathrm{p}},\delta,p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}},\mathbf{U}_{g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}}\bigr).

(16)

This expression describes a coherent, parallel execution: for each computational basis state
\ket{\mathbf{x}_{\setVbnsfn}}
of the received register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
, the generic maximization unitary
\mathbf{U}_{\max}
(from Proposition
6
) is applied to the local registers of
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
.
The inverse operation,
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}^{\mathsf{H}}
, is constructed by reversing the stages of Algorithm
2
and taking the Hermitian conjugate of each unitary.

4.3
Performance Analysis of the
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
Construction

The resource costs and formal properties of the
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
construction (Algorithm
2
) are summarized below.

Lemma 8

(Performance of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
Construction)
.

The distributed implementation of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
has the following properties:

1.

Query Complexity:
One application of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
requires a total of

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot(2^{t(p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}},\delta)+2}-2)\in O\Biggl(N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}\sqrt{|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}}|}\Biggr)

(17)

queries to the fundamental oracles in
\{\mathbf{U}_{g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}}\}_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\,\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}
and their inverse across all worker QPUs.

2.

EPR Pair Consumption:
The protocol consumes a total of
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}(2|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|+N_{\mathrm{p}})
EPR pairs for quantum teleportation.

3.

State Preparation Guarantees:
The resulting state
\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}
satisfies the required overlap and accuracy conditions given in (
4
) and (
5
), which are necessary for the main algorithm’s performance (detailed in Theorem
5
).

Proof.

See Appendix
J
.
∎

5
Performance Guarantees and Resource Analysis

This section synthesizes the results from the preceding sections to provide a complete, end-to-end performance analysis of our distributed quantum algorithm,
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
.
Section
3
established the algorithm’s theoretical power assuming access to the state preparation unitary
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
. Recall that Section
4
then detailed the distributed protocol for constructing this unitary.
We now integrate the abstract performance of the main algorithm with the concrete implementation costs (derived in Lemma
8
and Appendix
F
) to quantify the framework’s true computational complexity and resource requirements.
This analysis establishes a holistic view of the algorithm’s practicality and scalability.

Setup
:
We analyze the distributed algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
from Algorithm
1
.
This analysis assumes each constituent unitary (
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
,
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{PE}}
, and
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{check}}
) is implemented by the distributed protocols detailed in Section
4
and Appendix
F
.
The performance guarantees depend on the precision parameters
\delta
and
N_{\mathrm{p}}
, the graph decomposition (
\{\mathsf{G}_{\mathrm{s},n_{\mathsf{G}}}\}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
,
\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}
), and the minimum success probabilities
p_{\min,\mathrm{c}}
and
p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}}
.

Theorem 9

(
Overall Algorithm Performance
)
.

The algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
, executed under the previously specified Setup, has the following resource requirements and performance guarantees.

1.

Total qubit count.

The total number of qubits in the network,
N_{\mathrm{qubits}}
, equals the sum of the register sizes in Table
3
(Appendix
D
).

2.

Qubits per processor.

The coordinator qubit count is

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{qubits}}(\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}})

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}|+t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+N_{\mathrm{p}}+N_{\mathsf{G}}\cdot(N_{\mathrm{p}}+1)+\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|

\displaystyle\quad+N_{\mathsf{G}}\cdot t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+3

\displaystyle\in O\Bigl(|\mathcal{V}|+\mathrm{Poly}\bigl(N_{\mathsf{G}},N_{\mathrm{p}},\log(\delta)\bigr)\Bigr).

The worker qubit count (for each
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
) is

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{qubits}}(\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}})

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}|+|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|+N_{\mathrm{p}}+t(p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}},\delta)+N_{\mathrm{ora},n_{\mathsf{G}}}+t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+1.

In particular, the dominant, problem-dependent term at worker
n_{\mathsf{G}}
scales with the local instance size

|\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}|
and its boundary interface
|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|
.

3.

Overall query complexity.

The total number of queries to the leaf oracles

\bigl\{\mathbf{U}_{g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}}\bigr\}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}],\,\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}
and their inverse (Definition
7
) is

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{distr}}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{N},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})

\displaystyle=N_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}\cdot\bigl(2^{\,t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+2}-2\bigr)\cdot\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\bigl(2^{\,t(p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}},\delta)+2}-2\bigr)

\displaystyle\in O\Biggl(\!N_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}\cdot(1-\delta)^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}N_{\mathsf{G}}}\cdot\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\sqrt{|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}|\cdot|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}}|}\Biggr),

(18)

which reflects a nested search: a global stage scaling with the boundary search space
|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}|
,
multiplied by parallel local stages scaling with
|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}}|
.

4.

Entanglement and communication.

For a single-hop topology (the coordinator is directly connected to all workers), the total number of EPR pairs consumed is

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{EPR}}

\displaystyle=N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot\bigl(2^{\,t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+2}-2\bigr)\cdot\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\bigl(2\,|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|+N_{\mathrm{p}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot\bigl(2^{t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+2}-4\bigr)\cdot N_{\mathsf{G}}+N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)\cdot(2N_{\mathsf{G}})

\displaystyle\in O\Bigl(N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot(1-\delta)^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}N_{\mathsf{G}}}\cdot|\mathcal{V}|\cdot\sqrt{|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}|}\Bigr).

(19)

Proof.

A detailed proof is provided in Appendix
K
.
∎

We make three remarks that clarify the interpretation of Theorem
9
.

Qubit scaling.
The theorem cleanly separates the coordinator and worker memory costs. The coordinator’s dominant problem-dependent load is governed by the boundary size
|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}|
and the number of workers
N_{\mathsf{G}}
, whereas worker
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
mainly scales with the size of its local subproblem,
|\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}|
. The partitioning trade-off is therefore explicit: a finer decomposition can reduce per-worker qubits, but only at the price of a larger coordination layer at
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
. In this sense, the separator is the key structural parameter that determines whether the problem can be distributed across small QPUs. Unlike circuit-cutting approaches, this reduction in per-QPU qubits does not rely on exponential classical reconstruction
[
30
,
31
]
.

Query complexity.
The product form in (
18
) reflects a nested search: an outer search over boundary assignments and, for each candidate boundary, parallel local maximization subroutines. The theorem therefore identifies two quantities that control the cost: the size of the boundary search space and the difficulty of the induced local problems. This is precisely where structure enters the analysis. Better partitions improve
p_{\min,\mathrm{c}}
and
p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}}
and thereby reduce both global and local costs. Compared with black-box search-space partitioning, which typically yields
O(\sqrt{KN})
query complexity on
K
processors
[
2
]
, the present scheme retains the Grover-type advantage up to explicit structure-dependent factors.

Communication cost.
Equation (
19
) isolates the entanglement cost of maintaining coherence across the network. For one call to
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
, the term
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}(2|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|+N_{\mathrm{p}})
counts the EPR pairs needed to distribute the boundary information and return the
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit local outputs. The theorem therefore makes the communication overhead explicit in the cut sizes
|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|
. This is important because query complexity and communication cost are distinct: an instance may have favorable oracle complexity while still being limited by network resources. Compared with circuit-cutting methods, the overhead here remains natively quantum and directly interpretable in terms of the chosen decomposition
[
30
,
31
]
.

5.1
Impact of Network Topology on Communication Overhead

The preceding analysis in Theorem
9
assumes an idealized, single-hop network where the coordinator can directly communicate with every worker.
Realistic DQC systems, however, will be built on physical networks with specific, and often constrained, topologies
[
10
,
3
]
.
In such scenarios, communication between non-adjacent processors relies on protocols such as
entanglement swapping
. Entanglement swapping consumes entangled resources at each intermediate node (or “hop”) to establish a long-distance entangled pair
[
37
,
9
]
, making communication a primary performance bottleneck.

We now generalize our analysis to an arbitrary connected network topology.
We show that the algorithm’s computational complexity remains invariant, but its communication cost scales linearly with the network’s diameter.
This result cleanly decouples the computational logic from the communication overhead and provides a quantitative tool for the co-design of distributed quantum algorithms and the physical networks on which they run.

Setup
:
Let
A_{\mathrm{dist}}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{N},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})
be the algorithm operating on an idealized single-hop (fully connected) network
\mathsf{N}
with
N_{\mathsf{G}}+1
processors.
Let
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{N}^{\prime}},\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{N}^{\prime}})
be any other quantum network that is a connected graph with at least
N_{\mathsf{G}}+1
processors.
We construct a corresponding algorithm,
A_{\mathrm{dist}}^{\prime}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{N}^{\prime},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})
, by mapping the coordinator
\mathrm{c}
and workers
n_{\mathsf{G}}
to distinct nodes in
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}
. All communication between non-adjacent nodes is implemented using multi-hop entanglement swapping.

Proposition 10

(
Performance on General Connected Networks
)
.

The algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist}}^{\prime}
on the general network
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}
has the following properties:

1.

Performance Invariance.

The algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist}}^{\prime}
achieves the same output guarantees as
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
.
Its total qubit count, query complexity to local oracles, success probability, and approximation precision are identical to those stated in Theorem
9
.

2.

Communication Cost Scaling.

The total number of EPR pairs consumed by
A_{\mathrm{dist}}^{\prime}
, denoted
N^{\prime}_{\mathrm{EPR}}
, is upper-bounded by

\displaystyle N^{\prime}_{\mathrm{EPR}}\leq d(\mathsf{N}^{\prime})\cdot N_{\mathrm{EPR}},

where
N_{\mathrm{EPR}}
is the consumption for the idealized network from (
19
).
The term
d(\mathsf{N}^{\prime})
is the coordinator-centric diameter of the network
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}
, defined as the maximum shortest-path distance from the coordinator to any worker:

\displaystyle d(\mathsf{N}^{\prime})\triangleq\max_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\bigl\{\mathrm{dist}_{\mathsf{N}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{c},n_{\mathsf{G}})\bigr\},

where
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathsf{N}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{c},n_{\mathsf{G}})
is the number of
edges
in the shortest path connecting nodes
\mathrm{c}
and
n_{\mathsf{G}}
in
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
L
.
∎

Remark 11
.

This proposition establishes the network diameter
d(\mathsf{N}^{\prime})
as a direct, linear “penalty factor” on the required entanglement resources. This leads to several practical design principles for DQC systems:

1.

Strategic Coordinator Placement.
In order to minimize communication overhead, the coordinator role should be assigned to a processor at a graph-theoretically central node of the network (i.e., one that minimizes the maximum distance to all other nodes).

2.

Topology-Aware Task Allocation.
For a fixed physical network, worker roles that are more communication-intensive (e.g., those with larger boundary sets
|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|
), are suggested to be assigned to physically closer QPUs to minimize
d(\mathsf{N}^{\prime})
.

3.

Network Design Principles.
This analysis provides a quantitative basis for preferring low-diameter topologies (e.g., star or fully-connected) over high-diameter ones (e.g., line) when designing dedicated DQC clusters.

The analysis in this paper assumes perfect, deterministic entanglement swapping.
In practice, entanglement swapping is probabilistic and degrades fidelity
[
36
]
.
The success probability and fidelity of an end-to-end entangled link decrease exponentially with the path length, which would further penalize high-diameter networks.
This provides a rich avenue for future research into the co-design of fault-tolerant distributed algorithms and realistic network topologies.

\triangle

5.2
Gate-Level State Vector Simulation

Figure 2:
Total number of leaf-oracle evaluations per execution of the distributed primitive versus the number of variables
|\mathcal{V}|
. The
y
-axis is logarithmic. The dashed curve is a log-linear fit (linear in
\log_{10}
).

Figure 3:
Total number of elementary EPR pairs consumed versus the coordinator-to-workers diameter (hops) under topology stretching. The
y
-axis is logarithmic.

Figures
2
–
3
provide an end-to-end gate-level
state vector validation of the distributed primitives analyzed in
Theorem
9
and
Proposition
10
.
In order to illustrate the speed-up of our proposed algorithm, we report two full-execution counters:
(i) total number of leaf-oracle evaluations (
i.e.
, oracle queries), and
(ii) total number of elementary EPR pairs consumed by teleportation-based coordination
[
4
]
.

Query scaling.

As shown in Fig.
2
,
the oracle-query count increases with the number of variable nodes
|\mathcal{V}|

and exhibits plateau–jump transitions.
In this sweep, the decomposition scales with problem size according to

|\mathcal{V}|=|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}|+N_{G}\cdot r
(with fixed
N_{G}
), so both the number of boundary nodes
|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}|
and the per-worker partition size
r
increase with
|\mathcal{V}|
(equivalently, both
2^{|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}|}
and
2^{r}
grow).
The jumps occur when the integer phase-estimation register length increases by one
(a ceiling of a logarithmic bound, via the function
t
in (
10
)),
which adds a controlled layer and increases the oracle-query counter by a constant multiplicative factor, as shown in Theorem
9
.
For comparison, we also plot a classical-communication benchmark (
cf.

[
2
]
) in which workers run local quantum maximum-finding and coordinate only through classical messages.
Our distributed quantum algorithm empirically uses fewer worker-oracle queries across the sweep.
This behavior is consistent with Theorem
9
, where global coherence avoids repeated boundary-conditioned local searches.

Topology-driven entanglement cost.

In Fig.
3
, we plot EPR consumption as the coordinator-to-workers hop diameter increases under different topologies.
For all topologies, total EPR consumption grows with diameter.
The EPR counter aggregates routed hop distances in our coordinator–worker entanglement-preserving execution mode, so different topologies can yield different totals even at the same diameter.
(Note that Proposition
10
provides a worst-case bound in terms of diameter, but does not characterize performance gaps among specific topologies.)

Appendix
M
reports the complete simulation setup and additional results.

5.3
Finding an Optimal Configuration

The primary algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
finds an approximation of the maximum value
g_{\max}
. However, many applications require the specific configuration
\mathbf{x}_{\max}
that produces this value. We now introduce an extension,
A_{\mathrm{dist,config}}
, that finds a specific configuration
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
whose value
g(\hat{\mathbf{x}})
is provably close to the true maximum.

The algorithm employs iterative self-reduction, a standard technique in complexity theory
[
1
]
. It determines the optimal assignment for each variable
x_{i}\in\mathcal{V}
one by one. In order to decide the value for a variable
x_{i}
, the main algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
runs twice: once with
x_{i}
fixed to
0
and once with
x_{i}
fixed to
1
. By comparing the two resulting value-approximations, the algorithm infers the optimal assignment for
x_{i}
, fixes that value, and proceeds to the next variable.

Setup
:
We use the primary algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist}}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{N},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})
(from Algorithm
1
) as a subroutine. This algorithm functions as an oracle that, with a success probability of at least
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}
, outputs an approximation of
g_{\max}
that satisfies the error bound in (
12
).

Theorem 12

(
Finding an Optimal Configuration
)
.

Given the Setup, there exists a distributed quantum algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist,config}}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{N},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})
that finds a configuration
\hat{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{X}
and has the following performance guarantees:

1.

Query Complexity.
The total query complexity to the fundamental local oracles is upper bounded by
(2|\mathcal{V}|+1)\cdot C_{\mathrm{distr}}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{N},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})\in O(2^{|\mathcal{V}|/2})
, where
C_{\mathrm{distr}}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{N},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})
is given in (
18
).

2.

Success Probability.
The algorithm succeeds with probability at least
(1-\delta)^{4N_{\mathrm{p}}|\mathcal{V}|+2N_{\mathrm{p}}}
. In order to ensure
(1-\delta)^{4N_{\mathrm{p}}|\mathcal{V}|+2N_{\mathrm{p}}}\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}
for any
0<\delta^{\prime}<1
, Bernoulli’s inequality implies that it suffices to choose

\delta\leq\delta^{\prime}/(4N_{\mathrm{p}}|\mathcal{V}|+2N_{\mathrm{p}}).

3.

Configuration Accuracy.
The output
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
satisfies

\displaystyle|g(\hat{\mathbf{x}})-g_{\max}|\leq 4\cdot|\mathcal{V}|\cdot(N_{\mathsf{G}}+1)\cdot 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

(20)

Proof.

See Appendix
N
.
∎

Remark 13
.

The performance of
A_{\mathrm{dist,config}}
scales linearly with the number of variables,
|\mathcal{V}|
, relative to the base algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
. This
O(|\mathcal{V}|)
factor arises because the self-reduction technique must iterate through the variables, invoking the
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
subroutine
2|\mathcal{V}|+1
times to make its decisions. The error bound (
20
) scales linearly with
|\mathcal{V}|
as the small approximation error from each of the
|\mathcal{V}|
decision steps can accumulate. The success probability compounds exponentially for this same reason.

\triangle

6
Divide-and-Conquer: A Hierarchical Framework for Scalable Distributed Optimization

The distributed algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
, presented in Section
3
, provides a robust method for solving optimization problems on factor graphs.
However, its applicability is bounded by a core assumption: each decomposed subproblem must fit within the resource limits of a single worker QPU.
As problem sizes grow, this assumption inevitably fails, presenting a hard barrier to scalability.
The qubit requirement for a sub-problem, established in Theorem
9
, scales with its size.
If this requirement exceeds the hardware’s physical limit,
N_{\mathrm{QPU},\max}
, the sub-problem becomes intractable for one device.

In order to bridge this gap between large-scale problems and the practical constraints of quantum networks, we introduce a hierarchical framework based on the divide-and-conquer paradigm
[
6
]
.
This framework acts as a recursive “quantum compiler,” systematically mapping a large logical problem onto a network of fixed-capacity quantum processors.
It treats any sub-problem exceeding hardware limits as a new, smaller optimization task to be solved by its own dedicated sub-network.
This strategy transforms our approach from a specialized tool into a universal paradigm, enabling it to tackle problems of arbitrary size by recursively applying the same distributed logic.

Recognizing the diverse capabilities of quantum hardware, we formalize two distinct execution modes for this framework.
These modes create a tunable trade-off,
i.e.
, a “performance dial”, between quantum advantage and hardware fidelity.

•

Mode 1: Coherent Cascade.
A fully coherent execution that preserves the global Grover-like speedup across the entire problem. This represents a powerful theoretical model for future fault-tolerant networks.

•

Mode 2: Hybrid Approach.
A hybrid quantum-classical execution that introduces intermediate measurements to reduce circuit depth. This practical strategy mitigates noise in near-term (NISQ) devices, albeit at the cost of increased query complexity.

6.1
The Hierarchical Strategy

The hierarchical strategy transforms the flat coordinator-worker topology into a recursive, tree-like structure.
The process involves two main phases: a top-down hierarchical decomposition (a classical pre-processing step) and a corresponding bottom-up hierarchical execution (the distributed quantum computation).

Top-Down Hierarchical Decomposition
.
In a classical pre-processing step, we recursively partition the problem until all computational tasks fit within the capacity of individual QPUs.
This process is formalized in Algorithms
3
, which establishes a consistent multi-index notation to track subgraphs across different levels of the hierarchy.

Algorithm 3

Top-Down Construction of the Hierarchical Decomposition

1:

procedure

Decompose
(
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}
,
\ell
,
N_{\mathrm{c},\max}
,
N_{\mathrm{w},\max}
)

2:

Using the qubit bounds in Theorem
9
, compute the coordinator and worker qubit requirements for
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}
, denoted by
N_{\mathrm{c},\mathrm{qubits}}(\mathbf{k})
and
N_{\mathrm{w},\mathrm{qubits}}(\mathbf{k})
.

3:

if

N_{\mathrm{c},\mathrm{qubits}}(\mathbf{k})\leq N_{\mathrm{c},\max}

and

N_{\mathrm{w},\mathrm{qubits}}(\mathbf{k})\leq N_{\mathrm{w},\max}

then

4:

Mark
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}
as a leaf at level
\ell
.

5:

return

6:

else

7:

Choose an internal boundary-variable set
\mathcal{V}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathrm{B},\mathbf{k}}
.

8:

Partition
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}
into child subgraphs
\{\mathsf{G}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathbf{k},k_{\ell+1}}\}_{k_{\ell+1}\in\mathcal{K}_{\ell+1}(\mathbf{k})}
.

9:

for
each
k_{\ell+1}\in\mathcal{K}_{\ell+1}(\mathbf{k})

do

10:

Decompose
(
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathbf{k},k_{\ell+1}}
,
\ell+1
,
N_{\mathrm{c},\max}
,
N_{\mathrm{w},\max}
)

11:

end

for

12:

end

if

13:

end

procedure

An
L
-level hierarchical decomposition of a factor graph
\mathsf{G}
is the rooted tree generated by Algorithm
3
, initialized with

\textsc{Decompose}(\mathsf{G},0,N_{\mathrm{c},\max},N_{\mathrm{w},\max}).

For notational simplicity, we adopt the convention that all leaves are regarded as lying at level
L
; if a branch terminates earlier, it may be extended by trivial copies of the same terminal subgraph. The resulting structure is characterized as follows:

1.

Level 0 (root):
The root of the tree is the original factor graph,

\mathsf{G}^{(0)}\triangleq\mathsf{G}.

2.

Meta-subgraphs:

Fix
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
and consider a non-leaf node
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell-1)}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
at level
\ell-1
, indexed by

\mathbf{k}^{\prime}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{\ell-1})\in[K_{1}]\times\cdots\times[K_{\ell-1}],

where
K_{1},\ldots,K_{\ell-1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Let

N_{\mathrm{c},\mathrm{qubits}}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})
and

N_{\mathrm{w},\mathrm{qubits}}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})

denote the coordinator and worker qubit requirements associated with
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell-1)}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
.
We call
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell-1)}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
a
meta-subgraph
if it violates the hardware constraints, that is,

N_{\mathrm{c},\mathrm{qubits}}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})>N_{\mathrm{c},\max}\quad\text{or}\quad N_{\mathrm{w},\mathrm{qubits}}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})>N_{\mathrm{w},\max}.

Such a node is partitioned by an internal boundary-variable set
\mathcal{V}^{(\ell)}_{\mathrm{B},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
into child subgraphs

\bigl\{\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},k_{\ell}}\bigr\}_{k_{\ell}\in\mathcal{K}_{\ell}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})},

where
\mathcal{K}_{\ell}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})
is a finite index set. We write

K_{\ell}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\triangleq|\mathcal{K}_{\ell}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})|
for the number of children of node
\mathbf{k}^{\prime}
.
In general, different meta-subgraphs may have different branching factors.

3.

Leaf-subgraphs:

The recursion terminates at nodes whose resource requirements satisfy the hardware constraints. Under the uniform-depth convention above, such terminal nodes are denoted by
\mathsf{G}^{(L)}_{\mathbf{k}}
and satisfy

N_{\mathrm{c},\mathrm{qubits}}(\mathbf{k})\leq N_{\mathrm{c},\max}\quad\text{and}\quad N_{\mathrm{w},\mathrm{qubits}}(\mathbf{k})\leq N_{\mathrm{w},\max}.

These leaves are the atomic subproblems executed on individual QPUs.

Bottom-Up Hierarchical Execution
.
Once the decomposition tree has been constructed, computation proceeds from the leaves toward the root. Each node of the tree is assigned a corresponding sub-network, with one QPU designated as the local coordinator for that node and the QPUs assigned to its children acting as the workers for the next higher level. At an internal node, the coordinator solves the associated subproblem by running a level-specific instance of
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
over its children. This requires a coherent implementation not only of the amplitude-amplification operator in (
8
), but also of the controlled phase-testing primitive in (
9
). As shown in Appendix
F
, the corresponding distributed protocol may require parent-child teleportation both for the child-dependent components of (
8
) and for the controlled-unitary mechanism inside (
9
). The distinction between coherent and hybrid execution is therefore a distinction about how these inter-level quantum primitives are realized.

Definition 14

(
Hierarchical Algorithm
)
.

Let
A_{\mathrm{hier}}
operate on the hierarchical decomposition produced by Algorithm
3
. Its execution is defined recursively as follows.

1.

Base case (level
L
).

For a leaf subgraph
\mathsf{G}^{(L)}_{\mathbf{k}}
, the assigned QPU runs the generic maximization routine
\mathbf{U}_{\max}
from Appendix
I
, conditioned on the boundary variables supplied by its parent. The output is an
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-qubit register encoding an
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit approximation of the corresponding local optimum.

2.

Recursive step (level
\ell<L
).

Consider an internal node
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}
with children

\{\mathsf{G}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathbf{k},k}\}_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{\ell+1}(\mathbf{k})}
.
Its local coordinator executes the single-level distributed routine
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
of Algorithm
1
, treating the child nodes as its workers. The key design choice is how the level-
\ell
inter-level primitives are implemented from the child routines. In particular, one must realize coherently or classically the child-dependent operations that appear in the level-
\ell
counterparts of (
8
) and (
9
).

Execution modes. For any sub-coordinator at level
\ell<L
:

3.

Mode 1: Coherent cascade.

The level-
\ell
coordinator invokes the child routines without intermediate measurement and maintains a coherent parent-child interface throughout the realization of the level-
\ell
counterparts of (
8
) and (
9
). Consequently, the child sub-network is used coherently not only to implement the level-
\ell
state-preparation, marking, and reflection operations, but also to support the controlled-unitary steps that occur inside the phase-testing subroutine. This preserves coherence across levels and retains the Grover-like quadratic speedup, but it also requires repeated teleportation of data and control qubits across the parent-child interface, deep circuits, and sustained high-fidelity inter-node entanglement.

4.

Mode 2: Hybrid with intermediate measurement.

Each child routine is measured after completion, and only the resulting classical bit strings are returned to the level-
\ell
