Each processor (
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
and each worker
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
) checks if all its local data qubits participating in the reflection are in the
\ket{0}
state. This is achieved by:

(a)

Applying Pauli-
\mathbf{X}
gates to all participating local data qubits.

(b)

Applying a multi-controlled Toffoli gate where the controls are all the aforementioned data qubits and the target is a local ancilla qubit (
q_{\mathrm{CZ},\mathrm{c}}
at
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
,
q_{\mathrm{CZ},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
at
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
), initially in the
\ket{0}
state.

(c)

Re-applying Pauli-
\mathbf{X}
gates to the data qubits to return them to their original state.

After this step, the local ancilla is flipped to
\ket{1}
if and only if all local data qubits were in the
\ket{0}
state.

3.2)

Gather Ancillas:
Each worker
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
teleports its ancilla qubit
q_{\mathrm{CZ},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
to
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
.

3.3)

Global Zero-State Check:

\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
performs a multi-controlled Toffoli gate where the control qubits are its own ancilla
q_{\mathrm{CZ},\mathrm{c}}
and all received ancillas from the workers. The target is the global ancilla qubit
q_{\mathrm{global\_zero}}
. This target qubit is flipped to
\ket{1}
if and only if all local ancillas were in the
\ket{1}
state, indicating that the entire distributed system is in the
\ket{\mathbf{0}}
state.

3.4)

Apply Global Phase:

\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
applies a
-\mathbf{Z}
gate to the global ancilla
q_{\mathrm{global\_zero}}
.
This imparts phases of
+1
and
-1
to the state components with
q_{\mathrm{global\_zero}}=\ket{1}
and
q_{\mathrm{global\_zero}}=\ket{0}
, corresponding to
the entire distributed system being in the
\ket{\mathbf{0}}
state and in an arbitrary non-zero state, respectively.

3.5)

Uncomputation:
The protocol is reversed to disentangle all ancillas and return them to the
\ket{\mathbf{0}}
state. This involves:

(a)

Reversing the global Toffoli gate from Step 3.3.

(b)

Teleporting the worker ancillas from
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
back to their respective
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
.

(c)

Reversing the local zero-state check from Step 3.1 at each processor.

F.2
Protocol for Distributed Controlled-Unitary Application

The QPE algorithm requires the application of controlled powers of a unitary operator. In order to structure this clearly and avoid repetition, we define a general protocol for applying a distributed unitary
\mathbf{U}
for
k
times, conditioned on a control qubit
q_{c}
(from the register
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}}
) at
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
.

Protocol 1: Distributed Controlled-
U^{k}
Application

•

Objective:
To implement the operation
C\text{-}\mathbf{U}^{k}
, where
\mathbf{U}
is a distributed unitary, and the control qubit
q_{c}
(from the register
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}}
) resides at
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
.

•

Procedure:

1.

Control Information Distribution:
For each worker
n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]
:

(a)

At
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
, apply a CNOT gate with
q_{c}
(from the register
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}}
) as the control and a local auxiliary qubit
q_{\mathrm{aux},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
(from the register
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
) as the target.

(b)

\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
sends the qubit
q_{\mathrm{aux},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
to
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
via quantum teleportation. This qubit now serves as the local control at
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
.

(c)

After all workers receive their control qubits,
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
broadcasts a classical synchronization signal, ‘CONTROL_DISTRIBUTED’.

2.

Synchronized Execution:
Upon receiving the signal, all processors execute the following loop for
j=1
to
k
:

(a)

All processors (coordinator and workers) apply their respective components of the distributed unitary
\mathbf{U}
. Every local quantum gate within this distributed operation is made conditional on the corresponding local control qubit (
q_{c}
at
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
,
q_{\mathrm{aux},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
at each
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
).

(b)

After completing one application of
C\text{-}\mathbf{U}
, all processors wait for a classical synchronization signal, ‘ITERATION_COMPLETE’, from
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
before proceeding to the next iteration (if
j<k
).

3.

Control Information Uncomputation:
After
k
iterations,
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
broadcasts a classical signal, ‘UNCOMPUTE_CONTROL’. The operations of Step 1 are then executed in reverse to disentangle and reset all auxiliary control qubits to the
\ket{\mathbf{0}}
state.

F.3
Distributed Implementation of the Approximately Reversible Subroutine

We now assemble the preceding components to realize the full distributed implementation of the approximately reversible subroutine from (
9
). The protocol coherently applies a phase estimation procedure conditional on the state of the prefix register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}(1:n_{\mathrm{p}}-1)
at
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
.

Protocol 2: Distributed Implementation of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe-sub}}\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)

•

Objective:
To implement the controlled unitary operation in (
9
) in a distributed and approximately reversible manner.

•

Procedure:

1.

Forward Quantum Phase Estimation (QPE):

(a)

Initialize Estimation Register (at
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
):
The coordinator applies a Hadamard transform,
\mathbf{H}^{\otimes t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)}
, to its local estimation register
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}}
.

(b)

Apply Controlled Unitaries:
For each qubit index
t^{\prime}
from
1
to
t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)
:

–

The network executes
Protocol 1
with the
t^{\prime}
-th qubit of
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}}
as the control qubit
q_{c}

and the
t^{\prime}
-th qubit of
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
as the local auxiliary qubit.

–

The target unitary
\mathbf{U}
is
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)}
. Its application is additionally controlled by the state of the prefix register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}(1:n_{\mathrm{p}}-1)
.
Note that only the implementation of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}}
needs this control register and
\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}}
is implemented by
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
,
i.e.
,
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
does not need to teleport any auxiliary qubits related to

\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}(1:n_{\mathrm{p}}-1)
to any worker QPU.

–

The number of repetitions is
k=2^{t^{\prime}-1}
.

2.

Local Phase Readout (at
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
):

(a)

Inverse Quantum Fourier Transform:

\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
applies the inverse QFT to the estimation register
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}}
.

(b)

Conditional Flip:

\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
applies a multi-controlled NOT gate that flips the target result qubit
q_{\mathrm{p,c}}(n_{\mathrm{p}})
if and only if the state of the estimation register
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}}
is not
\ket{\mathbf{0}}
. This is implemented by applying Pauli-
\mathbf{X}
gates to all qubits in
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}}
, followed by a standard multi-controlled Toffoli gate targeting
q_{\mathrm{p,c}}(n_{\mathrm{p}})
, and finally re-applying the Pauli-
\mathbf{X}
gates to
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}}
.

3.

Uncomputation for Reversibility:
In order to ensure the subroutine is approximately reversible, the operations of the forward QPE are undone.

(a)

Reverse Phase Readout:

\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
applies the forward QFT to
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}}
(reversing Step 2(a)).

(b)

Reverse QPE:
For each qubit index
t^{\prime}
from
t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)
down to
1
(in reverse order), the network executes the inverse of
Protocol 1
.

(c)

Reverse Initialization (at
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
):
The coordinator applies a Hadamard transform,
\mathbf{H}^{\otimes t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)}
, to its local estimation register
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}}
.

4.

Error mitigation
: For the first invocation,
i.e.
, for
n_{\mathrm{p}}=1
, the ancilla qubit
q_{\mathrm{ancilla}}
is initialized to
\ket{0}
and is returned to
\ket{0}
after every application of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}}
by Step 2.4 of the marking-oracle implementation. By Theorem
24
, for
n_{\mathrm{p}}\geq 2
, the same reset property is guaranteed on the clean component inherited from the previous invocations, namely the component on which the prefix register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}(1:n_{\mathrm{p}}-1)
stores the correct prefix and all workspace qubits have been restored to their zero states after implementing
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe-sub}}\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)
. On this clean component,
q_{\mathrm{ancilla}}
is again available in the state
\ket{0}
and can therefore be reused as the auxiliary qubit required in the present step. The quantum network then executes the distributed zero-state-check procedure from Section
F.1.3
on all qubits involved in Protocol 2 except
q_{\mathrm{p,c}}(n_{\mathrm{p}})
and
q_{\mathrm{ancilla}}
. The procedure is identical to that in Section
F.1.3
, except that Step
3.4)
is replaced by the following operation:

3.4)

Conditioned on
q_{\mathrm{global\_zero}}
being in the state
\ket{0}
, the coordinator
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
applies a SWAP gate to the pair
\bigl(q_{\mathrm{p,c}}(n_{\mathrm{p}}),q_{\mathrm{ancilla}}\bigr)
. On the clean component entering this step, where
q_{\mathrm{ancilla}}
is in the state
\ket{0}
, this maps
\ket{1}_{q_{\mathrm{p,c}}(n_{\mathrm{p}})}\ket{0}_{q_{\mathrm{ancilla}}}
to
\ket{0}_{q_{\mathrm{p,c}}(n_{\mathrm{p}})}\ket{1}_{q_{\mathrm{ancilla}}}
, while leaving
\ket{0}_{q_{\mathrm{p,c}}(n_{\mathrm{p}})}\ket{0}_{q_{\mathrm{ancilla}}}
unchanged. Equivalently, this step is triggered precisely when the joint state of all qubits participating in the zero-state test is not the all-zero state.

Combining Item
2
and Item
4
of Theorem
24
, each execution of the phase-testing primitive
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe-sub}}\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)
admits a clean component in which the decision qubit stores the correct outcome and all workspace qubits, including
q_{\mathrm{ancilla}}
, are returned to their zero states. For a single execution, this clean component has norm at least
1-\delta
, and hence probability at least
(1-\delta)^{2}
.

Therefore,
q_{\mathrm{ancilla}}
can be reused across successive executions of (
9
) on the clean component. More precisely, after the first
n_{\mathrm{p}}
invocations, the component on which the prefix register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}(1:n_{\mathrm{p}})
stores the correct
n_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit prefix and
q_{\mathrm{ancilla}}
is restored to
\ket{0}
after each invocation has norm at least
(1-\delta)^{n_{\mathrm{p}}}
and therefore probability at least
(1-\delta)^{2n_{\mathrm{p}}}
. The same argument applies to the other workspace registers that are uncomputed on the clean component.

This sequence of protocols provides a complete and feasible distributed implementation of the core subroutine. The explicit enumeration of steps, including classical synchronization signals and the handling of distributed control, demonstrates that the proposed algorithm is well-defined within the established model of distributed quantum computation.

Appendix G
Proof of Theorem
5

This appendix provides a rigorous proof for the three performance guarantees of the distributed quantum algorithm asserted in Theorem
5
. We will establish each of the three claims of the theorem in turn: the query complexity, the success probability and outcome correctness, and the approximation precision.

The core of the algorithm is an iterative application of a phase-testing subroutine to a sequence of amplitude amplification operators. This structure is used to perform a binary search for the optimal value
g_{\max}
. The proof’s foundation therefore rests on analyzing the spectral properties of the amplitude amplification operators and leveraging the performance guarantees of the underlying phase-testing subroutine, as detailed in Appendix
E
.

G.1
Proof of Item
1
(Query Complexity to
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
)

The query complexity is determined by the total number of applications of the state preparation oracle
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
and its inverse
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}^{\mathsf{H}}
. The algorithm proceeds through an iterative loop that executes for
N_{\mathrm{p}}
steps, one for each bit of the final approximation.

In each iteration
n_{\mathrm{p}}\in[N_{\mathrm{p}}]
, Step
6
of Algorithm
1
executes the approximately reversible phase-testing subroutine
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe-sub}}\left(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot\right)
from Definition
23
. The core unitary operator for this subroutine is
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}\triangleq\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)}
, and the initial state preparation is performed by
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe,0}}=\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
.

From Definition
4
, a single application of the operator
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}
requires one application of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
and one application of its inverse
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}^{\mathsf{H}}
. According to Theorem
24
(Item
1
), a single execution of the subroutine
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe-sub}}\left(\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe,0}},\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}},p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta\right)
requires
2^{t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+1}-2
controlled applications of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}
and one application of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe,0}}
and its inverse.

Therefore, the total number of applications of the
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
and its inverse per iteration index by
n_{\mathrm{p}}\in[N_{\mathrm{p}}]
is given by

\displaystyle 2\cdot\left(2^{t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+1}-2\right)+2=2^{t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+2}-2.

Summing this cost over all
N_{\mathrm{p}}
iterations gives the total query complexity to the
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
oracle and its inverse as

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot\left(2^{t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+2}-2\right).

This completes the proof of the query complexity.

G.2
Proof of Item
2
(Success Probability and Outcome)

The proof of the success probability and outcome correctness relies on the properties of the phase-testing subroutine from Theorem
24
. In order to apply this theorem, we first establish two key lemmas regarding the spectral properties of the amplitude amplification operator
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}}
.

Lemma 26

(Eigen-decomposition for the amplitude-amplification operator)
.

Fix
n_{\mathrm{p}}\in[N_{\mathrm{p}}]
and a prefix threshold
\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{n_{\mathrm{p}}}
. Let

\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}=\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}}

be the amplitude-amplification operator from Definition
4
. We define the projector onto the marked subspace by

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathbf{z}}\triangleq\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{rem}}\otimes\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}},

and define the corresponding weight of the prepared state by

\displaystyle p_{\mathbf{z}}\triangleq\left\|\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathbf{z}}\cdot\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\in[0,1].

We assume that
0<p_{\mathbf{z}}<1
, and define

\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\triangleq 2\arcsin\bigl(\sqrt{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\bigr)\in(0,\pi).

Then
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}
has two orthonormal eigenvectors

\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}
and

\overline{\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}}

with eigenvalues
\mathrm{e}^{\pm\iota\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}}
, respectively, and the prepared state satisfies

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\iota\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}/2}\cdot\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}+\mathrm{e}^{-\iota\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}/2}\cdot\overline{\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}}\right).

Proof.

By Definition
4
and (
8
), it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}\cdot\bigl(2\ket{\mathbf 0}\bra{\mathbf 0}-\mathbf{I}\bigr)_{\mathrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}^{\mathsf{H}}\cdot\bigl(\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{rem}}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}}\bigr).

We define

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\psi}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}\cdot\bigl(2\ket{\mathbf 0}\bra{\mathbf 0}-\mathbf{I}\bigr)_{\mathrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}^{\mathsf{H}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{z}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{rem}}\otimes\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}}.

Then
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}=\mathbf{R}_{\psi}\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{z}}
, where

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\psi}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}\cdot\Bigl(2\ket{\mathbf 0}\bra{\mathbf 0}\Bigr)_{\mathrm{all}}\cdot\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}^{\mathsf{H}}-\mathbf{I}\overset{(a)}{=}2\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}\bra{\psi_{\rmini}}-\mathbf{I},

and step
(a)
follows from the state-preparation specification (
3
) (absorbing the always-zero clean ancillas into
\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}
) and the unitarity of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
. Moreover, since
\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}}
applies a phase of
-1
exactly to the marked computational-basis states, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{z}}=\mathbf{I}-2\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathbf{z}}.

(30)

Since
0<p_{\mathbf{z}}<1
, we define

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathbf{z}}\cdot\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}}{\sqrt{p_{\mathbf{z}}}},\qquad\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}\triangleq\frac{(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathbf{z}})\cdot\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}}{\sqrt{1-p_{\mathbf{z}}}}.

These two vectors are normalized and orthogonal because
\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathbf{z}}
is a projector and
\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathbf{z}})=\mathbf{0}
. Furthermore, it holds that

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathbf{z}}\cdot\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}+(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathbf{z}})\cdot\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}+\sqrt{1-p_{\mathbf{z}}}\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}

\displaystyle=\sin\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}/2\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}+\cos\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}/2\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}},

(31)

where the last equality uses
\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}=2\arcsin(\sqrt{p_{\vz}})
.

We now analyze the action of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}
on the two-dimensional subspace

\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{z}}\triangleq\mathrm{span}\Bigl\{\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}},\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}\Bigr\}.

By (
30
), it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{z}}\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}

\displaystyle=\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}},\qquad\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{z}}\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}=-\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}.

Using
\mathbf{R}_{\psi}=2\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}\bra{\psi_{\rmini}}-\mathbf{I}
together with (
31
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\psi}\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\Bigl(2\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}\bra{\psi_{\rmini}}-\mathbf{I}\Bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}2\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}\innerproduct{\psi_{\rmini}}{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}-\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}2\cos(\phi_{p_{\vz}}/2)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}-\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{=}\Bigl(2\cos^{2}(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}/2)-1\Bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}+2\sin(\phi_{p_{\vz}}/2)\cdot\cos(\phi_{p_{\vz}}/2)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(e)}{=}\cos\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}+\sin\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}},

where step
(a)
follows from the definition of
\mathbf{R}_{\psi}
, where step
(b)
follows from linearity, where step
(c)
follows from (
31
) and orthonormality of
\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}
and
\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}
, where step
(d)
follows from substituting (
31
), and where step
(e)
follows from the trigonometric identities
2\cos^{2}(\theta)-1=\cos(2\theta)
and
2\sin(\theta)\cos(\theta)=\sin(2\theta)
. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\psi}\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}2\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}\innerproduct{\psi_{\rmini}}{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}-\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}2\sin(\phi_{p_{\vz}}/2)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}-\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\sin\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}-\cos\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}},

where step
(a)
follows from the same expansion, where step
(b)
follows from (
31
), and where step
(c)
follows from substitution and the same trigonometric identities. Therefore, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}

\displaystyle=\cos\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}+\sin\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}},

(32)

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}

\displaystyle=-\sin\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}+\cos\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}.

(33)

Hence,
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{z}}
is invariant under
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}
, and in the ordered orthonormal basis

\Bigl(\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}},\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}\Bigr),

the restriction of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}
to
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{z}}
is the rotation matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\cos\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\bigr)&-\sin\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\bigr)\\
\sin\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\bigr)&\cos\bigl(\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}\bigr)\end{pmatrix}.

We define

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\Bigl(\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}-\iota\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}\Bigr),

\displaystyle\overline{\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\Bigl(\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}+\iota\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}\Bigr).

Since
\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}
and
\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}
are orthonormal, the vectors
\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}
and
\overline{\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}}
are also orthonormal. Using (
32
)–(
33
), we compute

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\Bigl(\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}-\iota\cdot\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\Bigl(\bigl(\cos(\phi_{p_{\vz}})+\iota\cdot\sin(\phi_{p_{\vz}})\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}-\iota\cdot\bigl(\cos(\phi_{p_{\vz}})+\iota\sin(\phi_{p_{\vz}})\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\mathrm{e}^{\iota\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\Bigl(\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}-\iota\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\iota\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}}\cdot\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}},

where step
(a)
follows from linearity, where step
(b)
follows from substituting (
32
)–(
33
), and where step
(c)
follows from
\cos\alpha+\iota\sin\alpha=\mathrm{e}^{\iota\alpha}
with
\alpha=\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}
. Similarly, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}\overline{\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\Bigl(\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}+\iota\cdot\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\Bigl(\bigl(\cos(\phi_{p_{\vz}})-\iota\cdot\sin(\phi_{p_{\vz}})\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}+\iota\cdot\bigl(\cos(\phi_{p_{\vz}})-\iota\sin(\phi_{p_{\vz}})\bigr)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\mathrm{e}^{-\iota\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\Bigl(\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}+\iota\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\iota\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}}\cdot\overline{\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}},

where step
(a)
follows from linearity, where step
(b)
follows from substituting (
32
)–(
33
), and where step
(c)
follows from
\cos\alpha-\iota\sin\alpha=\mathrm{e}^{-\iota\alpha}
with
\alpha=\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}
.
Finally, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{e}^{\iota\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}/2}\cdot\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}+\mathrm{e}^{-\iota\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}/2}\cdot\overline{\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{1}{2}\Biggl(\mathrm{e}^{\iota\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}/2}\cdot\Bigl(\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}-\iota\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}\Bigr)+\mathrm{e}^{-\iota\phi_{p_{\mathbf{z}}}/2}\cdot\Bigl(\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}+\iota\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}\Bigr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle\overset{(b)}{=}\cos(\phi_{p_{\vz}}/2)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{bad}}}+\sin(\phi_{p_{\vz}}/2)\cdot\ket{\psi_{\vz}^{\mathrm{good}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(c)}{=}\ket{\psi_{\rmini}},

where step
(a)
follows from the definitions of
\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}
and
\overline{\ket{\phi_{\rmini}}_{\mathbf{z}}}
, where step
(b)
uses
\mathrm{e}^{\iota\theta}+\mathrm{e}^{-\iota\theta}=2\cos\theta
and
\mathrm{e}^{-\iota\theta}-\mathrm{e}^{\iota\theta}=-2\iota\sin\theta
, and where step
(c)
follows from (
31
). This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma
26
shows that, for each prefix threshold
\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{n_{\mathrm{p}}}
with
0<p_{\mathbf{z}}<1
, the phase-testing instance arising in Theorem
5
fits the abstract setup of Definition
21
under the identifications

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe,0}}=\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}},\qquad\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{o}}=\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},\mathsf{N},\mathbf{z}},\qquad p_{\min}=p_{\min,\mathrm{c}}.

Moreover, Lemma
27
below shows that whenever
p_{\mathbf{z}}>0
, we have
p_{\mathbf{z}}\geq p_{\min,\mathrm{c}}
, so the lower-bound requirement in Definition
21
is satisfied in the nondegenerate case. Therefore, Theorem
24
can be applied to each phase-testing call used in the proof of Theorem
5
. The case
p_{\mathbf{z}}=0
is covered by Item
2
of Theorem
24
.

Lemma 27
.

If
p_{\mathbf{z}}>0
for some
\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{n_{\mathrm{p}}}
, then
p_{\mathbf{z}}\geq p_{\min,\mathrm{c}}
.

Proof.

The condition
p_{\mathbf{z}}>0
implies that the state
\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}
has a non-zero projection onto the subspace marked by
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}}
. This means there exists at least one configuration
\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}
such that the probability of measuring this configuration in the registers
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
is strictly positive. Let
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
be the configuration defined as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\in&\arg\max\limits_{\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}}\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})\\
&\qquad\ \mathrm{s.t.}\quad\bra{\psi_{\rmini}}\Bigl(\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{rem}}\otimes\ket{ \mathbf{z}_{\rmp,[\Nsfn]} }\bra{ \mathbf{z}_{\rmp,[\Nsfn]} }\Bigr)\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}>0\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}=(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}\in\prod_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\{0,1\}^{N_{\mathrm{p}}}\end{aligned}\quad.

(34)

By definition,
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
is the configuration with the highest value of
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})
among all configurations that have a non-zero measurement probability. If
p_{\mathbf{z}}>0
, it must be that
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]})\geq\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}^{\prime})
for some
\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}
. Since
\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}
, we have
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}^{\prime})\geq z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z})
. Thus, we have
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]})\geq z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z})
, which implies that
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}
. From (
4
), the probability of measuring
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
is at least
p_{\min,\mathrm{c}}
. Since
p_{\mathbf{z}}
is the total probability of measuring any state in
\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}
, it must be at least as large as the probability of measuring the single state
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
. Therefore, we have
p_{\mathbf{z}}\geq p_{\min,\mathrm{c}}
.
∎

We now prove Item
2
of Theorem
5
. The proof proceeds by induction on the number of iterations,
n_{\mathrm{p}}
, to demonstrate that the iterative procedure results in a quantum state that has a large amplitude associated with an ideal state encoding the correct solution. This ideal state after
k
iterations is defined as

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{ideal},k}}\triangleq\ket{\bm{0}}_{\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{loc},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{aux}}}\ket{ \mathbf{z}_{\max,\rmp,\rmc}(1:k) }_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}(1:k)},

(35)

where
k\in[N_{\mathrm{p}}]
.
Here, the state
\ket{\bm{0}}
signifies that all registers other than the result register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}
are returned to their initial all-zero state, and the state
\ket{ \mathbf{z}_{\max,\rmp,\rmc}(1:k) }
represents the first
k
bits of the best
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit lower approximation of the target value, as defined in expression (
11
).

Inductive Hypothesis
P(n_{\mathrm{p}})

Let
P(n_{\mathrm{p}})
be the inductive hypothesis for an iteration
n_{\mathrm{p}}\in[N_{\mathrm{p}}]
. After
n_{\mathrm{p}}
iterations of the algorithm, the system is in a state
\ket{\Psi^{(\np)}}
. The hypothesis
P(n_{\mathrm{p}})
asserts that the squared magnitude of the projection of this state onto the ideal state from expression (
35
) is lower-bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\left|\innerproduct{\psi_{\mathrm{ideal},\np}}{\Psi^{(\np)}}\right|^{2}\geq(1-\delta)^{2n_{\mathrm{p}}}.

This inequality states that the probability of a measurement on the relevant registers yielding the outcome corresponding to the ideal state
\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{ideal},\np}}
is at least
(1-\delta)^{2n_{\mathrm{p}}}
.

Base Case (
n_{\mathrm{p}}=1
)

For the base case, we establish that the hypothesis
P(1)
holds. This step corresponds to the algorithm’s first iteration, which determines the most significant bit of the solution,
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}(1)
. This is equivalent to testing whether the true value
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})
is greater than or equal to
0.5
. The analysis is divided into two exhaustive cases.

•

Case 1:
Suppose that
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})\geq 0.5
. This condition implies that the best 1-bit lower approximation is
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}(1)=1
, which corresponds to the set of outcomes
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\geq(1)}
. By the definition of
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
in (
34
), the initial state
\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}
must have a non-zero projection onto this set. Therefore, the probability
p_{(1)}
is strictly positive,

\displaystyle p_{(1)}=\left\|(\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{rem}}\otimes\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq(1)}})\ket{ \psi_{\rmini} }\right\|_{2}^{2}>0.

From Lemma
27
, it follows that this probability is lower-bounded by
p_{(1)}\geq p_{\min,\mathrm{c}}>0
. According to Theorem
24
and Lemma
26
, the resulting system state
\ket{\Psi^{(1)}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Bigl|\underbrace{\bigl(\bra{\bm{0}}_{\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{loc},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{aux}}}\bra{1}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}(1)}\bigr)}_{=\bra{\psi_{\mathrm{ideal},1}}}\ket{\Psi^{(1)}}\Bigr|^{2}=\left|\innerproduct{\psi_{\mathrm{ideal},1}}{\Psi^{(1)}}\right|^{2}\geq(1-\delta)^{2}.

This satisfies the condition for
P(1)
, since
(1-\delta)^{2}\geq(1-\delta)^{2\cdot 1}
.

•

Case 2:
Suppose that
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})<0.5
. In this case, the best 1-bit lower approximation is
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}(1)=0
. This condition implies that no component of the state
\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}
corresponds to an outcome in
\mathcal{Z}_{\geq(1)}
. Therefore, the probability
p_{(1)}
is exactly zero,

\displaystyle p_{(1)}=\left\|(\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{rem}}\otimes\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq(1)}})\ket{ \psi_{\rmini} }\right\|_{2}^{2}=0.

According to Theorem
24
and Lemma
26
, when the phase is zero, the system state
\ket{\Psi^{(1)}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Bigl|\underbrace{\bigl(\bra{\bm{0}}_{\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{loc},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{aux}}}\bra{0}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}(1)}\bigr)}_{=\bra{\psi_{\mathrm{ideal},1}}}\ket{\Psi^{(1)}}\Bigr|^{2}=\left|\innerproduct{\psi_{\mathrm{ideal},1}}{\Psi^{(1)}}\right|^{2}=1.

This also satisfies the condition for
P(1)
, since
1\geq(1-\delta)^{2}
.

In both cases, the hypothesis
P(1)
holds.

Inductive Step

Assume that the inductive hypothesis
P(k)
holds for an arbitrary integer
k
such that
1\leq k<N_{\mathrm{p}}
. Our objective is to prove that
P(k+1)
also holds.

By the inductive hypothesis
P(k)
,

\left|\innerproduct{\psi_{\mathrm{ideal},k}}{\Psi^{(k)}}\right|\geq(1-\delta)^{k}.

Equivalently, the state
\ket{\Psi^{(k)}}
contains a clean component of norm at least
(1-\delta)^{k}
in which all registers other than
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}(1:k)
are returned to their all-zero states and the register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}(1:k)
stores the correct prefix
\ket{\mathbf{z}_{\max,\rmp,\rmc}(1:k)}
. In the
(k+1)
-th iteration, the algorithm applies the same phase-testing subroutine as in the base case, now conditioned on this
k
-bit prefix. Accordingly, the relevant threshold string is obtained by appending the bit
1
to the correct prefix, namely

\bigl(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}(1:k),1\bigr).

Therefore, the analysis of the
(k+1)
-th step is the same as in the base case, with the test threshold replaced by
z_{\mathrm{dec}}\bigl(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}(1:k),1\bigr)
.

Let
\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}}\triangleq\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}(1:k)
. The algorithm tests whether the true value is greater than or equal to
z_{\mathrm{dec}}\bigl((\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)\bigr)
. This is determined by the value of the probability
p_{(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)}
.

•

Case 1:
Suppose that
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})\geq z_{\mathrm{dec}}\bigl((\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)\bigr)
. This condition implies that the
(k+1)
-th bit of the solution is
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}(k+1)=1
. Following the same logic as in the base case, this leads to
p_{(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)}>0

and according to Theorem
24
and Lemma
26
, the resulting system state
\ket{\Psi^{(k+1)}}
satisfies
\left|\innerproduct{\psi_{\mathrm{ideal},k+1}}{\Psi^{(k+1)}}\right|^{2}\geq(1-\delta)^{2(k+1)}.

•

Case 2:
Suppose that
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})<z_{\mathrm{dec}}\bigl((\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)\bigr)
. This condition implies that the
(k+1)
-th bit of the solution is
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}(k+1)=0
. Following the same logic as in the base case, this leads to
p_{(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)}=0

and according to Theorem
24
and Lemma
26
, the resulting system state

\ket{\Psi^{(k+1)}}
satisfies
\left|\innerproduct{\psi_{\mathrm{ideal},k+1}}{\Psi^{(k+1)}}\right|^{2}\geq(1-\delta)^{2k}.

In each iteration, the probability of correctly determining the next bit is at least
(1-\delta)^{2}
. The total success probability after
(k+1)
iterations is the product of the success probability after
k
iterations and the success probability of the
(k+1)
-th step. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\left|\innerproduct{\psi_{\mathrm{ideal},k+1}}{\Psi^{(k+1)}}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left|\innerproduct{\psi_{\mathrm{ideal},k}}{\Psi^{(k)}}\right|^{2}\cdot(1-\delta)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(1-\delta)^{2k}\cdot(1-\delta)^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\delta)^{2(k+1)}.

This shows that if
P(k)
is true, then
P(k+1)
is also true.

Conclusion

We have established the base case
P(1)
and have shown that the inductive step holds. By the principle of mathematical induction, the hypothesis
P(n_{\mathrm{p}})
is true for all
n_{\mathrm{p}}\in[N_{\mathrm{p}}]
. For
n_{\mathrm{p}}=N_{\mathrm{p}}
, this implies that the final state of the algorithm
\ket{\Psi_{\mathrm{final}}}\equiv\ket{\Psi^{(\Np)}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\innerproduct{\psi_{\mathrm{ideal},\Np}}{\Psi_{\mathrm{final}}}\right|^{2}\geq(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

This completes the proof of Item
2
.

G.3
Proof of Item
3
(Approximation Precision)

The total approximation error,
g_{\max}-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}})
, can be decomposed into two distinct components: the state preparation error and the quantization error. We can write the total error as

\displaystyle g_{\max}-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}})

\displaystyle\quad=\left(g_{\max}-\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})\right)+\left(\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}})\right).

We now bound each of these terms.

State Preparation Error:

The first term,
g_{\max}-\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})
, represents the error inherent in the state preparation oracle
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
. This error arises because each local maximization algorithm
\mathbf{U}_{\max}
(used within the implementation of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
) produces an
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit approximation of the local maximum, not the exact value. As established in (
5
), which will be proven in Lemma
8
, this error is bounded as

\displaystyle 0\leq g_{\max}-\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})\leq N_{\mathsf{G}}\cdot 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

(36)

Quantization Error:

The second term,
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}})
, is the error introduced by approximating the real-valued sum
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})
with a finite,
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit binary fraction. By the definition of
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}
in expression (
11
) as the best
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit lower approximation of this sum, this error is strictly non-negative and bounded above by the resolution of the
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit representation. Specifically, we have

\displaystyle 0\leq\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}})<2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

(37)

Total Error Bound:

Combining the bounds from inequalities (
36
) and (
37
), we obtain the bound on the total error:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq g_{\max}-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}})<(N_{\mathsf{G}}\cdot 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}})+2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}=(N_{\mathsf{G}}+1)\cdot 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

This completes the proof of the approximation precision.

Appendix H
Transforming Classical Circuits to Unitary Operations

The simulation involves two main steps:

1.

Converting Classical Logic to Reversible Quantum Circuits:
Any classical computation, including irreversible logical gates, can be transformed into an equivalent reversible circuit, typically by adding ancilla bits to store intermediate results that would otherwise be overwritten. Each gate in this reversible classical circuit can then be replaced by a corresponding universal quantum gate (
e.g.
, Toffoli gates). This construction ensures that the quantum circuit size is polynomially related to the classical circuit size.

2.

Simulating Classical Randomness with Quantum Superposition:
Classical randomness can be simulated by introducing auxiliary qubits initialized to
\ket{\mathbf{0}}
and applying Hadamard gates to create uniform superpositions. These qubits can then control different computational branches, mimicking random choices. More general probability distributions can also be prepared.

This principle is formally captured by the complexity class inclusion
\mathrm{BPP}\subseteq\mathrm{BQP}

[
5
]
, indicating that problems solvable by bounded-error probabilistic polynomial-time classical algorithms are also solvable by bounded-error quantum polynomial-time algorithms. For a detailed construction, see,
e.g.
,
[
29
, Chapter 4.5]
.

Appendix I
A Generic Quantum Maximization Algorithm

This appendix provides the detailed construction of the generic quantum algorithm for function maximization, which serves as a fundamental building block for the distributed protocol in Section
4
. The algorithm finds an
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit approximation of the maximum value of an arbitrary function
F:\{0,1\}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
by leveraging quantum phase estimation and amplitude amplification in an iterative binary search. (For details of quantum phase estimation and amplitude amplification, see,
e.g.
,
[
29
]
.)

Algorithm 4

Quantum Algorithm for Maximization (Generic
F
)

1:

State preparation oracle
\mathbf{U}_{F}
, marking oracles
\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{F\geq\mathbf{z}}}
, precision bits
N_{\mathrm{p}}
, error parameter
\delta\in(0,1)
, amplitude lower bound
p_{\min,F}>0
.

2:

Register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},F}
holds the information about
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},F}
, the best
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit lower approximation of
F^{\max}
.

3:

Initialize
all registers to
\ket{\mathbf{0}}
:
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{1st},F},\,\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},F},\,\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},F},\,\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},F})\leftarrow\ket{\mathbf{0}}
.

4:

for

n_{\mathrm{p}}=1,\ldots,N_{\mathrm{p}}

do

5:

Determine bit
n_{\mathrm{p}}
via controlled phase-testing:
Execute the subroutine from expression (
38
) on registers
\bigl(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{1st},F},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},F},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},F},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},F}(1:n_{\mathrm{p}})\bigr)
. This operation tests the phase of the amplitude amplification operator
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},F,(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)}
and writes the binary outcome to the qubit
q_{\mathrm{p},F}(n_{\mathrm{p}})
.

6:

end

for

The high-level procedure is outlined in Algorithm
4
.
The formal specification of this procedure is as follows.

Definition 28

(Quantum Algorithm for Maximization (Generic
F
))
.

Consider the setup in Section
4.1
. The algorithm in Algorithm
4
aims to produce an
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit string
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},F}
representing the largest
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit number whose decoded value does not exceed

F^{\max}=\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{N}}F(\mathbf{y}).

Equivalently,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},F}\triangleq\arg&\max\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p}}\in\{0,1\}^{N_{\mathrm{p}}}\end{subarray}}z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p}})\\
&\qquad\!\!\text{s.t. }z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{p}})\leq F^{\max}\end{aligned}\quad.

The algorithm uses the registers
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{1st},F}
(with
t(p_{\min,F},\delta)
qubits),
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},F}
(with
N_{\mathrm{p}}
qubits),
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},F}
(with
N
qubits), and
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},F}
(with
N_{F}
ancilla qubits used to implement the state-preparation oracle
\mathbf{U}_{F}
). All registers are initialized to
\ket{\mathbf{0}}
. The oracle
\mathbf{U}_{F}
acts on
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},F},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},F})
and satisfies (
13
).

For each iteration
n_{\mathrm{p}}\in[N_{\mathrm{p}}]
, the algorithm applies an approximately reversible phase-testing step conditioned on the current prefix stored in
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},F}(1:n_{\mathrm{p}}-1)
. This controlled operation is

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}}\in\{0,1\}^{n_{\mathrm{p}}-1}}\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe-sub}}\left(\mathbf{U}_{F},\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},F,(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)},p_{\min,F},\delta\right)\otimes\ket{\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}}}\bra{\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}}}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},F}(1:n_{\mathrm{p}}-1)},

(38)

where, for each prefix
\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}}\in\{0,1\}^{n_{\mathrm{p}}-1}
, the amplitude-amplification operator is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},F,(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)}&\triangleq\mathbf{U}_{F}\cdot\bigl(2\ket{\mathbf{0}}\bra{\mathbf{0}}-\mathbf{I}\bigr)_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},F},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},F}}\cdot\mathbf{U}_{F}^{\mathsf{H}}\cdot\bigl(\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{F\geq(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)}}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},F}}\bigr),\end{split}

and the phase-flip oracle
\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{F\geq(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)}}
acts on the search register according to

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{F\geq(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)}}\ket{\vect{y}}\triangleq\begin{cases}-\ket{\vect{y}}&\text{if }F(\mathbf{y})\geq z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)\\
\ket{\vect{y}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\quad,

where
z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}},1)
denotes the decoded value of the
n_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit string obtained by appending the bit
1
to the prefix
\mathbf{z}_{\mathrm{prefix}}
. The subroutine
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe-sub}}
(defined in Appendix
E
) uses
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{1st},F}
as its estimation register,
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},F},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},F})
as its target register, and
q_{\mathrm{p},F}(n_{\mathrm{p}})
as its output qubit.

Repeating this controlled phase test for
n_{\mathrm{p}}=1,\ldots,N_{\mathrm{p}}
yields the unitary operator

\mathbf{U}_{\max}(N_{\mathrm{p}},\delta,p_{\min,F},\mathbf{U}_{F}).

The corresponding final state is defined by

\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{final},F}}\triangleq\mathbf{U}_{\max}(N_{\mathrm{p}},\delta,p_{\min,F},\mathbf{U}_{F})\cdot\ket{\mathbf{0}}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{1st},F},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},F},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},F},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},F}}.

\triangle

Theorem 29

(
Properties of Quantum Maximization Algorithm (Generic
F
)
)
.

Let
F:\{0,1\}^{N}\to[0,1]
and consider the algorithm in Definition
28
. Suppose that the state-preparation oracle
\mathbf{U}_{F}
satisfies (
13
). Then the following statements hold.

1.

Query complexity.
The total number of queries to the state-preparation oracle
\mathbf{U}_{F}
and its inverse
\mathbf{U}_{F}^{\mathsf{H}}
is

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot\bigl(2^{t(p_{\min,F},\delta)+2}-2\bigr).

2.

Success probability and output.
Measuring all registers of the final state
\ket{\psi_{\mathrm{final},F}}
yields the clean output state

\ket{\mathbf{0}}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{1st},F},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},F},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},F}}\ket{\mathbf{z}_{\max,\rmp,F}}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},F}}

with probability at least
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}
. Equivalently,

\displaystyle\bigl|

\displaystyle\bra{0}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{1st},F},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},F},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},F}}\bra{ \mathbf{z}_{\max,\rmp,F} }_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},F}}\ket{ \psi_{\mathrm{final},F} }\bigr|^{2}\geq(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

If one requires
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}
for some
0<\delta^{\prime}<1
, then by Bernoulli’s inequality it suffices to choose
\delta\leq\delta^{\prime}/(2N_{\mathrm{p}})
.

3.

Approximation precision.
The returned approximation
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},F}
satisfies

0\leq F^{\max}-z_{\mathrm{dec}}\bigl(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},F}\bigr)\leq 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

Proof.

The proof follows the same bit-by-bit induction as the proof of Theorem
5
, specialized to the single-function setting.

For Item
1
, each of the
N_{\mathrm{p}}
iterations invokes one instance of the approximately reversible phase-testing subroutine
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe-sub}}\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)
. By Item
1
of Theorem
24
, one such invocation uses
2^{t(p_{\min,F},\delta)+1}-2
applications of the amplitude-amplification operator and two uses of the initialization oracle
\mathbf{U}_{F}
or
\mathbf{U}_{F}^{\mathsf{H}}
. Since each application of the amplitude-amplification operator
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA},F,\mathbf{z}}
contains one call to
\mathbf{U}_{F}
and one call to
\mathbf{U}_{F}^{\mathsf{H}}
, the total number of queries per iteration is

2\bigl(2^{t(p_{\min,F},\delta)+1}-2\bigr)+2=2^{t(p_{\min,F},\delta)+2}-2.

Multiplying by
N_{\mathrm{p}}
gives the claimed query complexity.

For Item
2
, the argument is identical to the inductive proof used for Theorem
5
: at iteration
n_{\mathrm{p}}
, the algorithm tests whether
F^{\max}
is at least the threshold associated with the candidate prefix obtained by appending the bit
1
. If the threshold is feasible, then the corresponding marked subspace has weight at least
p_{\min,F}
by (
13
); otherwise the marked weight is zero. In the first case, Item
4
of Theorem
24
guarantees that the correct branch is produced with probability at least
(1-\delta)^{2}
, while in the second case Item
2
gives the correct outcome with probability
1
. Repeating this argument over
N_{\mathrm{p}}
iterations shows that the final clean output state is obtained with probability at least
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.

Finally, Item
3
follows from the construction of
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},F}
as the largest
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit lower approximation of
F^{\max}
. By definition,
z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},F})\leq F^{\max}
, and the spacing between adjacent
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit numbers in
[0,1]
is
2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}
. Therefore, the gap between
F^{\max}
and its largest
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit lower approximation is at most
2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.
∎

Appendix J
Proof of Lemma
8

The proof is structured to address each of the three claims in Lemma
8
sequentially.

We will establish the query complexity, the EPR pair consumption, and the state preparation fidelity by leveraging the formal procedures outlined in Algorithm
2
and the performance guarantees of the generic maximization algorithm from Theorem
29
.

J.1
Proof of Item 1 (Query Complexity to Local Oracles)

The query complexity of the unitary operator
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
is determined by the total number of calls to the fundamental local oracles
\bigl\{\mathbf{U}_{g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}},\mathbf{U}_{g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}}^{\mathsf{H}}\bigr\}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}],\,\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}
. The distributed procedure in Algorithm
2
reveals that these oracles are invoked exclusively within Stage 2 in Algorithm
2
. This step consists of the parallel application of the generic quantum maximization algorithm,
\mathbf{U}_{\max}
, by each of the
N_{\mathsf{G}}
worker processors, coherently controlled by the qubits
\bigl(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}},(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\bigr)
corresponding to the boundary variable configuration
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}
.

According to Item
1
of Theorem
29
, a single execution of
\mathbf{U}_{\max}(N_{\mathrm{p}},\delta,p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}},\mathbf{U}_{g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}})
requires
N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot\bigl(2^{t(p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}},\delta)+2}-2\bigr)
queries to its respective local oracle and its inverse. Since these maximization procedures are executed in parallel for each of the
N_{\mathsf{G}}
subgraphs, the total query complexity for one coherent application of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
is the sum of the queries made by each worker processor. This sum is given by

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot\left(2^{t(p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}},\delta)+2}-2\right),

which validates the expression in expression (
17
).

J.2
Proof of Item 2 (EPR Pair Consumption)

The consumption of EPR pairs within the
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
protocol is entirely attributable to the quantum teleportation operations required for inter-processor communication. We can account for the total consumption by itemizing the teleportation events as described in Algorithm
2
.

•

Distribution Phase:
In Stage 1 in Algorithm
2
, the coordinator
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
teleports the auxiliary register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
, which contains
|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|
qubits, to each worker processor
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
. The total number of EPR pairs consumed during this distribution phase is the sum over all workers, which amounts to
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|
.

•

Collection Phase:
In Stage 3 in Algorithm
2
, each worker processor
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
teleports its registers
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
(containing
|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|
qubits) and
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
(containing
N_{\mathrm{p}}
qubits) back to the coordinator
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
. The total number of EPR pairs consumed during this collection phase is therefore
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}(|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|+N_{\mathrm{p}})
.

The total EPR pair consumption for a single application of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
is the sum of the costs from both phases. This sum is

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|+\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}(|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|+N_{\mathrm{p}})=\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}(2|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|+N_{\mathrm{p}}),

thereby confirming the expression for EPR pair consumption.

J.3
Proof of Item 3 (State Preparation Fidelity and Accuracy)

We prove separately the overlap bound (
4
) and the accuracy bound (
5
).

Proof of (
4
) (weight on the optimal outcome).

Let
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}^{\ast}
be an optimal boundary assignment attaining
g_{\max}
, and let

\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}=\bigl(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}}\bigr)_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}

be the associated tuple of local
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit approximations introduced below (
3
).

After Stage 1 of Algorithm
2
, the boundary register is in the uniform superposition

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}|}}\sum_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}}\ket{\vx_{\setVb}}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}}}.

For each worker
n_{\mathsf{G}}
and each boundary assignment
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}
, define

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\nsfn,\vx_{\setVb}}}\triangleq\mathbf{U}_{\max}\bigl(N_{\mathrm{p}},\delta,p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}},\mathbf{U}_{g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}}\bigr)\cdot\ket{\mathbf{0}}_{\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},n_{\mathsf{G}}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},n_{\mathsf{G}}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}.

Since Stage 2 applies these local maximization unitaries coherently and in parallel, and Stage 3 only teleports
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
back to the coordinator and then uncomputes
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
, the state prepared by
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
can be written as

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}|}}\sum_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}}\ket{\vx_{\setVb}}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}}}\otimes\bigotimes_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\ket{\psi_{\nsfn,\vx_{\setVb}}}.

(39)

In particular, on the optimal branch
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}^{\ast}
, the worker states factorize across
n_{\mathsf{G}}
.

For each worker
n_{\mathsf{G}}
, we define the corresponding clean success state by

\displaystyle\ket{\chi_{\nsfn}^{\ast}}\triangleq\ket{\mathbf{0}}_{\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},n_{\mathsf{G}}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},n_{\mathsf{G}}},\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}\ket{\mathbf{z}_{\max,\rmp,\nsfn}}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}.

By Item
2
of Theorem
29
, applied to the local function

g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}^{\ast}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\innerproduct{\chi_{\nsfn}^{\ast}}{\psi_{\nsfn,\vx_{\setVb}^{\ast}}}\right|^{2}\geq(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}},\qquad\forall\,n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}].

(40)

Therefore, the amplitude of the specific global branch in which the boundary register equals
\ket{\vx_{\setVb}^{\ast}}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}}}
and every worker is in its clean success state is

\displaystyle\left|\left(\bra{\vx_{\setVb}^{\ast}}_{\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}}}\otimes\bigotimes_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\bra{\chi_{\nsfn}^{\ast}}\right)\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}\right|^{2}\overset{(a)}{=}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}|}\prod_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\left|\innerproduct{\chi_{\nsfn}^{\ast}}{\psi_{\nsfn,\vx_{\setVb}^{\ast}}}\right|^{2}\overset{(b)}{\geq}\frac{(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}N_{\mathsf{G}}}}{|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}|},

where step
(a)
follows from (
39
), and where step
(b)
follows from (
40
). Since the projector

\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{rem}}\otimes\mathbf{P}_{\{\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\}}

retains every component whose value registers equal

\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}
, its expectation is at least the probability weight of this particular branch. Hence,

\displaystyle\bra{\psi_{\rmini}}\bigl(\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{rem}}\otimes\mathbf{P}_{\{\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\}}\bigr)\ket{\psi_{\rmini}}\geq\frac{(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}N_{\mathsf{G}}}}{|\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}|}=p_{\min,\mathrm{c}}.

This proves (
4
).

Proof of (
5
) (accuracy of the encoded value).

Let
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}^{\ast}
be as above. Then

\displaystyle g_{\max}=\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}^{\ast}}^{\max}.

For each
n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]
, Item
3
of Theorem
29
, applied to the local maximization problem with boundary assignment
\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}}^{\ast}
, yields

\displaystyle 0\leq g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}^{\ast}}^{\max}-z_{\mathrm{dec}}\bigl(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}}\bigr)\leq 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

Summing over
n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]
gives

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}^{\ast}}^{\max}-\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}z_{\mathrm{dec}}\bigl(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}}\bigr)=g_{\max}-\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}z_{\mathrm{dec}}\bigl(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}}\bigr)\leq N_{\mathsf{G}}\cdot 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

This is exactly (
5
). The proof of Item 3 is complete.

Appendix K
Proof of Theorem
9

The proof of Theorem
9
establishes the overall performance and resource requirements of the distributed quantum algorithm presented in Algorithm
1
. The proof is a synthesis of previously established results. It combines the abstract performance guarantees of the main algorithmic structure, as proven in Theorem
5
, with the concrete resource costs of its distributed components. Specifically, we integrate the properties of the state preparation unitary
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
, proven in Lemma
8
, and the distributed implementations of the reflection and controlled-phase-estimation operators, which are detailed in Appendix
F
. Each item of the theorem is proven sequentially below.

K.1
Item
1
and Item
2
: Total Qubit Count and Allocation per Processor

The total number of qubits and their allocation to each processor are derived by enumerating all quantum registers defined in Appendix
D
and accounting for their physical location throughout the algorithm’s execution. The analysis distinguishes between registers that are statically allocated to a processor and those that are transient, being transferred via quantum teleportation during the protocol.

K.1.1
Qubits Processed by the Coordinator Processor (
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
)

The coordinator processor manages a set of registers throughout the algorithm. The maximum number of qubits that it needs to simultaneously control, determines its hardware requirement.

•

Statically Allocated Registers:
These registers reside permanently at
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
.

–

Boundary variable register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{B}}
:
|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}|
qubits.

–

Coordinator processing registers
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{cen}}
, which include the phase estimation register
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}}
and the final result register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p,c}}
:
t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+N_{\mathrm{p}}
qubits.

–

Ancilla qubits for the distributed reflection operator,
q_{\mathrm{CZ},\mathrm{c}}
and
q_{\mathrm{global\_zero}}
:
2
qubits.

•

Transiently Held Registers:
These registers are teleported to
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
from the worker processors at specific stages of the algorithm.

–

Local result registers
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
, received from workers for implementing the marking oracle
\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}}
and for the final uncomputation step of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
:
N_{\mathsf{G}}\cdot N_{\mathrm{p}}
qubits.

–

Auxiliary registers for distributing control information,
(\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
:
N_{\mathsf{G}}\cdot t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)
qubits.

–

Auxiliary boundary registers
(\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
, received from workers during the uncomputation step of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
:
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|
qubits.

–

Ancilla qubits for the distributed reflection,
(q_{\mathrm{CZ},n_{\mathsf{G}}})_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
, received from workers to perform the multi-controlled gate:
N_{\mathsf{G}}
qubits.

–

An ancilla qubit
q_{\mathrm{ancilla}}
for implementing
\mathbf{U}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\geq\mathbf{z}}}
and
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{pe-sub}}\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)
in a distributed manner.

The total number of qubits processed by
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
is the sum of the sizes of these registers, which yields the expression in Item
2
of the theorem.

K.1.2
Qubits Processed by a Worker Processor (
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
)

Each worker processor
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
manages its own set of local and auxiliary registers.

•

Statically Allocated Registers:

–

Local processing registers
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{loc},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
, which include the local phase estimation register
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
, the internal variable register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{2nd},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
, and the local result register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{p},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
:
t(p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}},\delta)+|\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}|+N_{\mathrm{p}}
qubits.

–

Local oracle ancilla register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{ora},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
:
N_{\mathrm{ora},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
qubits.

•

Transiently Held Registers:

–

Auxiliary boundary register
\mathbf{q}_{\mathrm{Baux},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
, received from
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
during the execution of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
:
|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|
qubits.

–

Copies of distributed control information,
(\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c},n_{\mathsf{G}}})
, received from
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
during the main phase estimation loop:
t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)
qubits.

–

An ancilla qubit for the distributed reflection,
q_{\mathrm{CZ},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
:
1
qubit.

Summing the sizes of these registers gives the total qubit count for each worker processor, as stated in Item
2
. The total qubit count for the entire network, stated in Item
1
, is the sum of the coordinator’s qubits and the qubits of all
N_{\mathsf{G}}
workers, taking care not to double-count the transient registers. This corresponds to the sum of all entries in Table
3
.

K.2
Item
3
: Overall Query Complexity

The overall query complexity is measured in the standard distributed model, where the fundamental cost is the number of calls to the local oracles
\bigl\{\mathbf{U}_{g_{n_{\mathsf{G}},\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}}\bigr\}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}],\,\mathbf{x}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}}}
and their inverse, which encode the problem structure. Other resource costs, such as communication, are analyzed separately. The derivation follows a two-level analysis that reflects the nested structure of the algorithm.

First, we consider the high-level complexity of the main algorithm described in Algorithm
1
. According to Item
1
of Theorem
5
, the main algorithm’s iterative structure requires a total of
N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot(2^{t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+2}-2)
applications of the state preparation unitary
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
and its inverse
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}^{\mathsf{H}}
.

Second, we consider the low-level complexity of a single application of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
. As established in Item 1 of Lemma
8
, each execution of the distributed procedure for
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
requires a total of
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot(2^{t(p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}},\delta)+2}-2)
queries to the fundamental local oracles and their inverses.

The total query complexity of the entire distributed algorithm is the product of these two quantities. This multiplicative relationship arises because the main loop repeatedly calls the
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
subroutine, and each of these subroutine calls in turn triggers a sequence of local oracle queries. Therefore, the total number of queries to the fundamental local oracles is

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{distr}}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{N},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})

\displaystyle=N_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}\cdot\bigl(2^{t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+2}-2\bigr)\cdot\Biggl(\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}\bigl(2^{t(p_{\min,n_{\mathsf{G}}},\delta)+2}-2\bigr)\Biggr),

which confirms the expression in (
18
).

K.3
Item
4
: Overall EPR Pair Consumption

The total number of EPR pairs consumed is the sum of pairs used in all quantum teleportation steps throughout the algorithm, assuming an idealized, single-hop quantum network. We derive this total by systematically accounting for the three distinct sources of EPR pair consumption.

K.3.1
Contribution from State Preparation and Uncomputation

The state preparation unitary
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
and its inverse
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}^{\mathsf{H}}
are called a total of
N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot(2^{t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+2}-2)
times during the execution of the main algorithm. According to Item 2 of Lemma
8
, each call consumes
\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}(2|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|+N_{\mathrm{p}})
EPR pairs for distributing and collecting the auxiliary boundary registers. The total consumption from this source is therefore

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{EPR},1}=N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot\bigl(2^{t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+2}-2\bigr)\cdot\Biggl(\sum_{n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]}(2|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},n_{\mathsf{G}}}|+N_{\mathrm{p}})\Biggr).

K.3.2
Contribution from the Distributed Reflection Operator

The reflection operator
(2\ket{\mathbf{0}}\bra{\mathbf{0}}-\mathbf{I})_{\mathrm{all}}
is a component of the amplitude amplification operator
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA}}
. The phase-testing subroutine, as detailed in Theorem
24
in Appendix
E
, in each of the
N_{\mathrm{p}}
iterations of the main algorithm makes
2^{t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+1}-2
calls to the controlled-
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{AA}}
operator. The distributed implementation of the reflection requires each of the
N_{\mathsf{G}}
workers to teleport its ancilla qubit
q_{\mathrm{CZ},n_{\mathsf{G}}}
to the coordinator and to receive it back, consuming
2N_{\mathsf{G}}
EPR pairs per reflection. The total consumption from this source is

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{EPR},2}=N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot\bigl(2^{t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)+1}-2\bigr)\cdot(2N_{\mathsf{G}}).

K.3.3
Contribution from Control Information Distribution

This cost arises from the implementation of the controlled-unitary operations within the phase-testing subroutine, as detailed in Appendix
F
. For each of the
N_{\mathrm{p}}
bits being determined, the algorithm executes a phase-testing subroutine that uses
t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)
control qubits from the register
\mathbf{q}_{1\mathrm{st},\mathrm{c}}
. For each of these control qubits, its quantum state is distributed to all
N_{\mathsf{G}}
workers and subsequently returned to uncompute the entanglement. This process requires
2N_{\mathsf{G}}t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)
teleportations per control qubit. The total consumption from this source is the product of the number of main iterations, the number of control qubits per iteration, and the communication cost per control qubit, which is

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{EPR},3}=N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)\cdot(2N_{\mathsf{G}}).

K.3.4
Total EPR Pair Consumption

The total number of EPR pairs consumed by the algorithm is the sum of these three contributions,
N_{\mathrm{EPR}}=N_{\mathrm{EPR},1}+N_{\mathrm{EPR},2}+N_{\mathrm{EPR},3}
. This sum yields the expression given in (
19
). We note that the dominant contribution to the EPR pair consumption arises from the repeated calls to
\mathbf{U}_{\mathrm{ini}}
and the distributed reflection operator, as both scale exponentially with the number of phase estimation qubits,
t(p_{\min,\mathrm{c}},\delta)
. This highlights the communication cost associated with the phase-testing subroutines as the primary bottleneck for the algorithm’s scalability in terms of network resources.

Appendix L
Proof of Proposition
10

The construction of the algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist}}^{\prime}
on the network
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}
follows directly from the implementation of
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
on
\mathsf{N}
, with modifications only to the communication primitives. The proof proceeds in three steps.

1. Remote Communication via Entanglement Swapping.

The original algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
requires communication between the coordinator
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
and each worker
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
. In an arbitrary connected network
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}
, these processors may not be directly connected. However, since
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}
is a connected graph, a path of length
k=\mathrm{dist}_{\mathsf{N}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{c},n_{\mathsf{G}})
exists between
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
and
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
,
where
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathsf{N}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{c},n_{\mathsf{G}})
is defined to be shortest-path distance from the coordinator to the worker
n_{\mathsf{G}}
in the network
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}
.
A single EPR pair can be established between these two remote processors by sequentially performing
k-1
entanglement swapping operations along this path
[
37
,
4
]
.
This process consumes one elementary EPR pair for each of the
k
links along the path.
Thus, teleporting a single qubit from
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
to
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}
(or vice versa), which consumes one EPR pair in a direct-link network, consumes
k=\mathrm{dist}_{\mathsf{N}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{c},n_{\mathsf{G}})
EPR pairs in the network
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}
.

2. Adapting the Algorithm and Aggregating Communication Costs.

The algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist}}^{\prime}
is constructed by replacing every single-hop quantum communication step in
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
(
e.g.
, the quantum teleportation steps in Algorithm
2
and Appendix
F
) with the corresponding multi-hop entanglement swapping protocol on
\mathsf{N}^{\prime}
.
Let
N_{\mathrm{EPR},i}
be the number of EPR pairs consumed by the
i
-th communication event in the original algorithm, where communication occurs between
\mathrm{QPU}_{\mathrm{c}}
and a worker
\mathrm{QPU}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}(i)}
.
In the adapted algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist}}^{\prime}
, this event now consumes
N^{\prime}_{\mathrm{EPR},i}=\mathrm{dist}_{\mathsf{N}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{c},n_{\mathsf{G}}(i))\cdot N_{\mathrm{EPR},i}
EPR pairs.
The total EPR pair consumption for
A_{\mathrm{dist}}^{\prime}
is the sum over all such events:

\displaystyle N^{\prime}_{\mathrm{EPR}}=\sum_{i}N^{\prime}_{\mathrm{EPR},i}=\sum_{i}\mathrm{dist}_{\mathsf{N}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{c},n_{\mathsf{G}}(i))\cdot N_{\mathrm{EPR},i}.

By the definition of the network diameter,
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathsf{N}^{\prime}}(\mathrm{c},n_{\mathsf{G}}(i))\leq d(\mathsf{N}^{\prime})
for all
i
.
Therefore, we can establish the upper bound:

\displaystyle N^{\prime}_{\mathrm{EPR}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i}d(\mathsf{N}^{\prime})\cdot N_{\mathrm{EPR},i}=d(\mathsf{N}^{\prime})\cdot\sum_{i}N_{\mathrm{EPR},i}=d(\mathsf{N}^{\prime})\cdot N_{\mathrm{EPR}}.

This proves the communication cost property.

3. Invariance of Computational Metrics.

The adaptation from
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
to
A_{\mathrm{dist}}^{\prime}
only modifies the implementation of communication between processors.
The sequence and nature of local quantum operations (gates, oracles, measurements) performed within each QPU remain unchanged.
Consequently, the total number of queries to local oracles, the number of qubits required at each processor, the overall success probability, and the final approximation precision are all independent of the network topology and remain identical to those specified in Theorem
9
.
This completes the proof.

Appendix M
Gate-level Wavefunction Validation: Query Scaling and Topology-Driven Entanglement Cost

Figure 8:
Total oracle queries per full execution of the distributed primitive versus the total number of variables
|\mathcal{V}|
(gate-level state vector simulation). The
y
-axis is logarithmic. Dashed lines are log-linear fits for both the proposed method and the classical-communication benchmark. Benchmark markers are all actual simulation points (no interpolation).

Figure 9:
Total EPR pairs consumed versus
|\mathcal{V}|
across canonical coordinator–worker topologies (gate-level state vector simulation). Here
|\mathcal{V}|
increases via the boundary-size schedule under fixed worker settings. Dashed curves are quadratic trend fits in log-space.

Figure 10:
Total EPR pairs consumed versus coordinator-to-workers diameter (hops) under topology stretching (gate-level state vector simulation).

Goal.

These experiments execute the same boundary-conditioned phase-estimation and amplitude-amplification control flow as the
distributed primitive, so they serve as an end-to-end check of the protocol assumptions used in
Theorem
9
and the topology accounting in
Proposition
10
. Two full-execution counters are considered:
(i) total leaf-oracle evaluations (oracle queries), and (ii) total elementary EPR pairs consumed under the
teleportation-and-swapping hop accounting.

Fig.
8
: query scaling and the coherence gap.

We sweep
|\mathcal{V}|\in\{8,9,\ldots,20\}
(13 simulated points) with two workers (
N_{\mathsf{G}}=2
). The baseline is a
classical-communication benchmark (
cf.

[
2
]
): workers run local quantum maximum-finding and exchange
only classical messages, with boundary-level repetition chosen to match the same precision and success targets as our
primitive. The coherent distributed primitive uses fewer oracle queries at every point, with a ratio that ranges from

1.37\times
to
302\times
over the sweep. Both curves show plateau–jump transitions because key protocol parameters
(
e.g.
, the phase-estimation register length) are integer-valued ceilings of logarithmic bounds: they remain constant over
intervals and increase by one at thresholds, producing discrete multiplicative increases in the oracle-query counter.

Fig.
9
: boundary growth separates topologies.

We fix
N_{\mathsf{G}}=6
,
|\mathcal{V}_{n_{\mathsf{G}}}|=1
for all
n_{\mathsf{G}}\in[N_{\mathsf{G}}]
,
N_{\mathrm{p}}=1
,
\delta=0.25
, and sweep

|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}|\in\{2,\ldots,8\}
(hence
|\mathcal{V}|\in\{8,\ldots,14\}
). The EPR cost increases rapidly with
|\mathcal{V}|
, and the
topology ordering is stable:

\text{line}>\text{ring}>\text{tree}>\text{mesh}>\text{star}.

The gap is systematic (
e.g.
, line is about
3.5\times
star across the sweep), which matches
Proposition
10
: at fixed algorithmic workload, EPR consumption is governed
by topology-dependent hop aggregation.

Fig.
10
: stretching isolates hop-geometry dependence.

We fix
|\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}|=5
,
N_{\mathsf{G}}=8
,
|\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}|=1
,
N_{\mathrm{p}}=1
,
\delta=0.25
, and increase hop distances by edge
stretching up to diameter
32
. This keeps the logical computation fixed and changes only routed distances. EPR
consumption increases monotonically with diameter and remains topology-separated, confirming that hop geometry is a
first-order driver. At matched diameter, aggregate coordinator-to-worker hop sums dominate, so diameter alone does not
predict the full ordering; under stretching, star becomes most expensive because every spoke scales with the stretch
(
e.g.
, at diameter
24
: star
17280
vs. line
9720
EPR pairs).

Summary.

The gate-level data align with the theory: coherent distributed execution reduces oracle-query volume relative to
classical communication only (Theorem
9
), while entanglement cost is controlled by
boundary size and topology-dependent hop aggregation (Proposition
10
).

Appendix N
Proof of Theorem
12

we assume, without loss of generality, that the variable set is
\mathcal{V}=\{1,2,\ldots,|\mathcal{V}|\}
and that each variable
x_{i}
is binary,
i.e.
,
x_{i}\in\{0,1\}
, consistent with the factor graph assumptions in this paper (see Section
2.2
).

Definition 30

(Algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist,config}}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{N},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})
)
.

The algorithm to find an optimal configuration
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
proceeds as follows:

Initial Step:

Run
A_{\mathrm{dist}}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{N},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})
on the original factor graph
\mathsf{G}
to obtain an
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit approximation
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(\mathrm{ref})}
of
g_{\max}
.
Let
\hat{g}_{\mathrm{ref}}=z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(\mathrm{ref})})
. Initialize an empty list for the configuration
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
.
For consistency, we define
