\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(0)}\triangleq\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(\mathrm{ref})}
.

Iterative Variable Fixing:

For each variable index
i\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{V}|\}
:

1.

Construct Modified Graphs and Evaluate:

For each possible assignment
x^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
for the current variable
x_{i}
:

•

Define a new factor graph
\mathsf{G}^{(i,x^{\prime})}
. This graph is identical to the original graph
\mathsf{G}
except that the alphabets of variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1}
are restricted to their previously determined optimal values
\{\hat{x}_{1}\},\ldots,\{\hat{x}_{i-1}\}
, and the alphabet of
x_{i}
is restricted to
\{x^{\prime}\}
. The alphabets of
x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{|\mathcal{V}|}
remain unchanged.

•

Let
g_{\max}^{(i,x^{\prime})}
be the true maximum of the global function corresponding to the factor graph
\mathsf{G}^{(i,x^{\prime})}
.

•

Run
A_{\mathrm{dist}}(\mathsf{G}^{(i,x^{\prime})},\mathsf{N},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})
to obtain an
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit approximation
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i,x^{\prime})}
of
g_{\max}^{(i,x^{\prime})}
. By Theorem
5
, with probability at least
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}
, this approximation satisfies:

\displaystyle\bigl|g_{\max}^{(i,x^{\prime})}-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i,x^{\prime})})\bigr|\leq\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}},

(41)

where
\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}\triangleq(N_{\mathsf{G}}+1)\cdot 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.

2.

Set Current Variable
\hat{x}_{i}
:

If

\displaystyle\Bigl|z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i,0)})-z_{\mathrm{dec}}\bigl(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i-1)}\bigr)\Bigr|\leq 2\cdot\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}},

(42)

then set
\hat{x}_{i}=0
. Otherwise, set
\hat{x}_{i}=1
.
Append
\hat{x}_{i}
to
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
and define
\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i)}\triangleq\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i,\hat{x}_{i})}
.

Output:
The algorithm outputs the configuration
\hat{\mathbf{x}}=(\hat{x}_{1},\ldots,\hat{x}_{|\mathcal{V}|})
.

\triangle

Now we prove the performance guarantee statements.

The algorithm
A_{\mathrm{dist,config}}
involves one initial call to
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
and then
2|\mathcal{V}|
subsequent calls to
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
during the iterative variable fixing stage (two calls for each of the
|\mathcal{V}|
variables).
Thus, there are a total of
1+2|\mathcal{V}|
calls to
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
.
The query complexity of
A_{\mathrm{dist}}(\mathsf{G}^{(i,x^{\prime})},\mathsf{N},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})
is at most
C_{\mathrm{distr}}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{N},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})
because fixing variables does not increase (and typically reduces) the size or complexity of the optimization problem. Thus the total query complexity to the fundamental local oracles is upper bounded by
(2|\mathcal{V}|+1)\cdot C_{\mathrm{distr}}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{N},\delta,N_{\mathrm{p}})\in O(2^{|\mathcal{V}|/2})
.

The probability that all these
1+2|\mathcal{V}|
calls to
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
succeed in providing approximations within the bound
\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}
(as specified in inequalities (
12
) for the initial call and (
41
) for subsequent calls) is at least
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot(1+2|\mathcal{V}|)}
.

Assuming all calls to
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
are successful (
i.e.
, their outputs satisfy the respective approximation bounds), we analyze the error accumulation.
Let
g_{\max}^{(i)}
denote the true maximum value of
g(\mathbf{x})
when variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{i}
are fixed to the determined values
\hat{x}_{1},\ldots,\hat{x}_{i}
, and the remaining variables
x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{|\mathcal{V}|}
are optimized.
Let
g_{\max}^{(0)}\triangleq g_{\max}
.
From the properties of
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
, we have
|g_{\max}^{(i)}-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i,\hat{x}_{i})})|\leq\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}
for all
i\geq 1
, and
|g_{\max}^{(0)}-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(0)})|\leq\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.
Consider the
i
-th step of variable fixing, where
\hat{x}_{i}
is determined.

Case 1:
\hat{x}_{i}=0
.

This assignment is chosen if the condition in inequality (
42
) holds:
|z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i,0)})-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i-1)})|\leq 2\cdot\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.
We bound the difference between the true maxima of consecutive optimally extended partial assignments:

\displaystyle\bigl|g_{\max}^{(i-1)}-g_{\max}^{(i,0)}\bigr|

\displaystyle\quad=\Bigl|\bigl(g_{\max}^{(i-1)}-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i-1)})\bigr)+\bigl(z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i-1)})-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i,0)})\bigr)+\bigl(z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i,0)})-g_{\max}^{(i,0)}\bigr)\Bigr|

\displaystyle\quad\overset{(a)}{\leq}\underbrace{\bigl|g_{\max}^{(i-1)}-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i-1)})\bigr|}_{\leq\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}}+\underbrace{\bigl|z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i-1)})-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i,0)})\bigr|}_{\leq 2\cdot\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}\text{ (by condition for this case)}}+\underbrace{\bigl|z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i,0)})-g_{\max}^{(i,0)}\bigr|}_{\leq\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}+2\cdot\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}+\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}

\displaystyle\quad=4\cdot\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}},

where step
(a)
follows from the triangle inequality. The subsequent inequality uses the approximation bounds from
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
and the condition for choosing
\hat{x}_{i}=0
.
Since
\hat{x}_{i}=0
in this case,
g_{\max}^{(i)}=g_{\max}^{(i,0)}
. Thus,
|g_{\max}^{(i-1)}-g_{\max}^{(i)}|\leq 4\cdot\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.

Case 2:
\hat{x}_{i}=1
.

This assignment is chosen if
\bigl|z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i,0)})-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i-1)})\bigr|>2\cdot\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.
Since
x_{i}
is binary, the true maximum
g_{\max}^{(i-1)}
(optimizing over
x_{i},\dots,x_{|\mathcal{V}|}
given
\hat{x}_{1},\dots,\hat{x}_{i-1}
) is equal to either
g_{\max}^{(i,0)}
(if
x_{i}=0
is optimal for the
i
-th variable) or
g_{\max}^{(i,1)}
(if
x_{i}=1
is optimal for the
i
-th variable).
Suppose, for contradiction, that
g_{\max}^{(i-1)}=g_{\max}^{(i,0)}
(
i.e.
, setting
x_{i}=0
would have been optimal). Then:

\displaystyle\bigl|z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i,0)})-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i-1)})\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\bigl|z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i,0)})-g_{\max}^{(i,0)}\bigr|+\bigl|g_{\max}^{(i,0)}-g_{\max}^{(i-1)}\bigr|+\bigl|g_{\max}^{(i-1)}-z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}_{\max,\mathrm{p},\mathrm{c}}^{(i-1)})\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}+0+\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}

\displaystyle=2\cdot\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

This contradicts the condition for setting
\hat{x}_{i}=1
. Therefore, our supposition is false, implying that
g_{\max}^{(i-1)}=g_{\max}^{(i,1)}
.
In this case, since
\hat{x}_{i}=1
, we have
g_{\max}^{(i)}=g_{\max}^{(i,1)}
.
Thus,
|g_{\max}^{(i-1)}-g_{\max}^{(i)}|=|g_{\max}^{(i,1)}-g_{\max}^{(i,1)}|=0\leq 4\cdot\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.

In both cases (whether
\hat{x}_{i}=0
or
\hat{x}_{i}=1
), we establish that for each step
i
:

\displaystyle|g_{\max}^{(i-1)}-g_{\max}^{(i)}|\leq 4\cdot\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

The total difference between the true global maximum
g_{\max}=g_{\max}^{(0)}
and the value of the function at the constructed configuration
g(\hat{\mathbf{x}})=g_{\max}^{(|\mathcal{V}|)}
can be bounded by a telescoping sum:

\displaystyle\Bigl|g_{\max}-g(\hat{\mathbf{x}})\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|g_{\max}^{(0)}-g_{\max}^{(|\mathcal{V}|)}\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\Biggl|\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{V}|}\bigl(g_{\max}^{(i-1)}-g_{\max}^{(i)}\bigr)\Biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{V}|}\bigl|g_{\max}^{(i-1)}-g_{\max}^{(i)}\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{V}|}(4\cdot\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}})

\displaystyle=4\cdot|\mathcal{V}|\cdot\Delta_{N_{\mathrm{p}}}

\displaystyle=4\cdot|\mathcal{V}|\cdot(N_{\mathsf{G}}+1)\cdot 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}.

This establishes the accuracy bound in inequality (
20
).

Appendix O
Proof of Theorem
15

We use the notation from the Setup and Theorem
15
.
In particular,
\mathcal{T}
denotes the decomposition tree, and each internal node
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}
is decomposed by
\mathcal{V}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathrm{B},\mathbf{k}}
into children
\{\mathsf{G}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathbf{k},k}\}_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{\ell+1}(\mathbf{k})}
.
Each such internal node runs a single-level instance of
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
(Algorithm
1
) on its children, and each leaf runs the local maximization routine
\mathbf{U}_{\max}
(Appendix
I
).
The proof consists of instantiating the single-level guarantees (Theorems
5
and
9
) at each node and composing them along the tree.

1. Qubits per processor (Item
1
).

Fix an internal node
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}
.
When its sub-coordinator executes
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
on its children, it plays the role of the coordinator in Theorem
9
, with the substitutions

N_{\mathsf{G}}\leftarrow K_{\ell+1}(\mathbf{k}),\qquad\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B}}\leftarrow\mathcal{V}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathrm{B},\mathbf{k}},\qquad\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{B},k}\leftarrow\mathcal{V}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathrm{B},\mathbf{k},k}.

Applying Item
2
of Theorem
9
and taking the dominant problem-dependent term yields the stated scaling in
\lvert\mathcal{V}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathrm{B},\mathbf{k}}\rvert
(up to factors polynomial in
K_{\ell+1}(\mathbf{k})
,
N_{\mathrm{p}}
, and
\log(1/\delta)
).
The leaf bound follows analogously from the worker expression in Item
2
.

2. Query complexity (Item
2
).

For each node
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathcal{T}
, let
C(\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}})
denote the total number of queries to the
leaf-level
local oracles incurred by one execution of the hierarchical subroutine rooted at
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}
.

Base case (leaf).

If
\ell=L
, then
\mathsf{G}^{(L)}_{\mathbf{k}}
executes
\mathbf{U}_{\max}
on local data.
By Proposition
6
, this contributes

C(\mathsf{G}^{(L)}_{\mathbf{k}})=C_{\mathrm{leaf}}(\mathsf{G}^{(L)}_{\mathbf{k}})

with
C_{\mathrm{leaf}}(\cdot)
as defined in (
22
).

Recursive case (internal node).

Let
\ell<L
.
If
\ell\notin\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{M}}
(coherent), then the level-
\ell
sub-coordinator executes
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
and, by Item
1
of Theorem
5
, makes

N_{\mathrm{p}}\cdot(2^{t(p_{\min,\mathbf{k}}^{(\ell)},\delta)+2}-2)
applications of its state-preparation oracle
\mathbf{U}^{(\ell)}_{\mathrm{ini}}
(and its inverse).
Each such application invokes
each
child subroutine once, hence

\displaystyle C(\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}})=C_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}})\cdot\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{\ell+1}(\mathbf{k})}C(\mathsf{G}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathbf{k},k}),

(43)

where
C_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}})
is given by (
24
).
If
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{M}}
(measured), the coordinator enumerates all boundary configurations, so the number of child invocations is
\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathrm{B},\mathbf{k}}}\rvert
, which again gives (
43
).
Unrolling the recursion from the root to the leaves yields

C_{\mathrm{hier}}=C(\mathsf{G}^{(0)})=\sum_{\mathsf{G}^{(L)}_{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathcal{T}}N_{\mathrm{inv}}(\mathsf{G}^{(L)}_{\mathbf{k}})\cdot C_{\mathrm{leaf}}(\mathsf{G}^{(L)}_{\mathbf{k}}),

with invocation counts
N_{\mathrm{inv}}(\cdot)
defined in (
23
).
Finally, the coherent big-
O
bound (
25
) follows by substituting (
21
) into the asymptotic bound in Item
1
of Theorem
5
.

3. EPR consumption (Item
3
).

Let
E(\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}})
be the total number of EPR pairs consumed by one execution of the hierarchical subroutine rooted at
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}
.
If
\ell=L
(leaf), then
E(\mathsf{G}^{(L)}_{\mathbf{k}})=0
under our accounting model because the computation is local.
If
\ell<L
(internal node), then one execution consists of:
(i) the level-
\ell
single-level routine
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
itself, which consumes
N_{\mathrm{EPR}}(\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}})
EPR pairs for coherent communication with the children (expression (
19
) instantiated with the substitutions above, and
N_{\mathrm{EPR}}(\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}})\triangleq 0
when
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{M}}
), and
(ii) the child subroutines, each invoked
C_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}})
times.
Therefore,

\displaystyle E(\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}})=N_{\mathrm{EPR}}(\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}})+C_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}})\cdot\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{\ell+1}(\mathbf{k})}E(\mathsf{G}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathbf{k},k}).

(44)

Unrolling (
44
) from the root yields the node-wise sum in Item
3
, where each node’s local EPR cost is weighted by its invocation count
N_{\mathrm{inv}}(\cdot)
.

4. Success probability and precision (Item
4
).

•

Coherent-cascade success.

We prove by induction on the level (from
L
up to
0
) that each coherent subroutine succeeds with probability at least
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.
For
\ell=L
, a leaf runs
\mathbf{U}_{\max}
, which succeeds with probability at least
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}
by Proposition
6
.
For the inductive step, fix an internal node
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}
with
\ell<L
executed coherently.
By the induction hypothesis, each child routine succeeds with probability at least
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.
Hence the success branch of the state-preparation oracle for
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}
has overlap at least

(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}\,K_{\ell+1}(\mathbf{k})}/\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{V}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathrm{B},\mathbf{k}}}\rvert
,
which is exactly
p_{\min,\mathbf{k}}^{(\ell)}
from (
21
).
Applying Item
2
of Theorem
5
to this level-
\ell
instance of
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
yields success probability at least
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.
The claim for the root follows.

•

Hybrid success.

In hybrid execution, success requires that every coherently executed subroutine whose output is subsequently read out, namely each node in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{out}}
from Theorem
15
, returns the correct
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit result across all its
N_{\mathrm{inv}}(\cdot)
invocations.
By the coherent-cascade argument above, each such coherent execution succeeds with probability at least
(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}}
(for leaves this is Proposition
6
, and for internal coherent nodes it follows from Item
2
of Theorem
5
applied at that node).
Therefore, by the chain rule for conditional probability,

\Pr(\text{success})\geq(1-\delta)^{2N_{\mathrm{p}}\,N_{\mathrm{exec}}}.

The sufficient choice of
\delta
for a target overall failure probability
\delta^{\prime}
then follows from Bernoulli’s inequality, as stated in Theorem
15
.

•

Additive-error certificate.

For each node
\mathsf{G}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}
, let
g_{\max}^{(\ell,\mathbf{k})}
denote the true optimum value of that subproblem, and let
\hat{g}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}\triangleq z_{\mathrm{dec}}(\mathbf{z}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}})
be the decoded value returned by the corresponding hierarchical subroutine upon success.
Define the additive error
\epsilon^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}\triangleq g_{\max}^{(\ell,\mathbf{k})}-\hat{g}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}\geq 0
.
For a leaf, Proposition
6
gives
\epsilon^{(L)}_{\mathbf{k}}\leq 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.
For an internal node, we decompose the error as in the single-level analysis (
cf.
(
5
) and the discussion around (
12
)):

\displaystyle\epsilon^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}

\displaystyle=\Biggl(g_{\max}^{(\ell,\mathbf{k})}-\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{\ell+1}(\mathbf{k})}\hat{g}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathbf{k},k}\Biggr)+\Biggl(\underbrace{\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{\ell+1}(\mathbf{k})}\hat{g}^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathbf{k},k}-\hat{g}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}}_{\overset{(a)}{\leq}2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}_{\ell+1}(\mathbf{k})}\epsilon^{(\ell+1)}_{\mathbf{k},k}+2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}},

(45)

where step
(a)
follows from the fact that
\hat{g}^{(\ell)}_{\mathbf{k}}
is, by construction of
A_{\mathrm{dist}}
(Item
2
of Theorem
5
), the best
N_{\mathrm{p}}
-bit lower approximation of the summed child values, and any such lower approximation has resolution at most
2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}
.
Applying (
45
) recursively down the tree shows that the root error is bounded by one
2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}}
contribution per node in
\mathcal{T}
, yielding

0\leq g_{\max}-\hat{g}^{(0)}\leq|\mathcal{T}|\cdot 2^{-N_{\mathrm{p}}},

which is the claimed precision bound.

Appendix P
Hierarchical Execution: Gate-level Policy Study (Queries and Entanglement)

Figure 11:
Total oracle-query count
Q_{\mathrm{total}}
versus
|\mathcal{V}|
(total number of variable nodes) for the coherent policy and three hybrid-policy benchmarks. The
y
-axis is logarithmic.

Figure 12:
Total EPR-pair count
E_{\mathrm{total}}
versus
|\mathcal{V}|
for the same benchmark. We plot only policies with nonzero coherent inter-node communication;
hybrid_all
is identically zero in this accounting model. The
y
-axis is logarithmic.

Goal and policies.

This experiment isolates a single architectural choice in the hierarchical framework of Section
6
: the placement of intermediate measurements. We compare the fully coherent policy (
coherent
) with three hybrid policies:
hybrid_root
(measurement at the root only),
hybrid_level1
(measurement at level 1 only), and
hybrid_all
(measurement at both internal levels). All policies use the same benchmark family, decomposition path, accuracy parameters, and accounting rules. The observed differences therefore isolate the effect of measurement placement itself.

Parameter setting (gate-level).

We use exact gate-level state-vector simulation with

\displaystyle(b_{0},b_{1},S_{0},S_{1},r)\in\{(5,0,1,1,r)\}_{r=3}^{5}\cup\{(5,1,1,1,r)\}_{r=5}^{8}\cup\{(6,1,1,1,r)\}_{r=8}^{13}

and fixed
(N_{\mathrm{p}},\delta,t_{\max})=(1,0.2,2)
.
Here
b_{0}
and
b_{1}
are boundary widths at levels 0 and 1, respectively,
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
are branching factors at levels 0 and 1, respectively, and
r
is the leaf-local bit width.
This monotone parameter path yields the contiguous sweep
|\mathcal{V}|=8,\ldots,20
while keeping the state dimension feasible for exact state-vector simulation. All reported oracle-query and EPR totals are extracted directly from the simulator’s gate-level counters, so the figures validate the implemented circuits.

Query ordering.

The observed ordering is

\texttt{coherent}<\texttt{hybrid\_root}<\texttt{hybrid\_level1}<\texttt{hybrid\_all}.

This ordering is exactly the behavior predicted by Item
2
. The total query count is built from the leaf cost in (
22
), weighted by the recursive invocation count in (
23
). In this two-level hierarchy,
hybrid_root
,
hybrid_level1
, and
hybrid_all
replace the coherent factor in (
24
) at the root only, at level 1 only, and at both internal levels, respectively. Each such replacement increases the number of lower-level calls, so the ordering is strict across the full sweep. At
|\mathcal{V}|=20
, the query multipliers relative to
coherent
are
4.27
,
2.55\times 10^{3}
, and
1.17\times 10^{4}
for
hybrid_root
,
hybrid_level1
, and
hybrid_all
, respectively.

Entanglement ordering.

The EPR accounting follows Item
3
, which counts only coherent inter-node communication and inherits the per-node cost from (
19
). Accordingly,
hybrid_all
is identically zero in this accounting model because both internal interfaces are measured. At
|\mathcal{V}|=20
, the EPR multipliers relative to
coherent
are
3.49
and
0.236
for
hybrid_root
and
hybrid_level1
, respectively. This contrast is important. Measuring at the root removes the root-level coherent primitive, but it also increases the number of coherent calls executed below the root; in the present benchmark, that replication dominates and leads to higher total EPR consumption. By contrast, measuring at level 1 removes the deeper coherent interface itself and therefore lowers the total EPR count. The figure thus confirms that Item
3
must be read as a node-wise sum weighted by invocation multiplicity, rather than as a purely local saving rule.

Interpreting the curve shapes.

To obtain a dense
contiguous
sweep in
|\mathcal{V}|
while keeping the state-vector dimension feasible, we vary the decomposition parameters
(b_{0},b_{1},S_{0},S_{1},r)
along a
monotone
path.
The resulting piecewise behavior is expected. Policies that keep a level coherent are sensitive to integer-valued circuit parameters and to discrete changes in boundary width, so their curves exhibit plateau–jump behavior. Policies that measure a level inherit explicit branching through (
24
); along the present benchmark path, this yields approximately log-linear growth whenever the dominant cost comes from enumeration.
Concretely, the sweep comprises three controlled regimes:

•

Regime A
(
|\mathcal{V}|=8,\ldots,10
): we fix
(b_{0},b_{1},S_{0},S_{1})=(5,0,1,1)
and increase only the leaf-local width
r
. This primarily enlarges the measured enumeration domain at the lower interface. Consequently,
hybrid_level1
and
hybrid_all
rise rapidly, whereas the coherent policy and
hybrid_root
remain nearly flat while the relevant integer circuit parameters stay unchanged.

•

Regime B
(
|\mathcal{V}|=11,\ldots,14
): we increase the level-1 boundary from
b_{1}=0
to
b_{1}=1
and continue increasing
r
. This changes the level-1 effective factor in (
24
) and also modifies the coherent communication cost inherited from (
19
), producing a visible step in the policies that still use or replicate the level-1 coherent primitive.

•

Regime C
(
|\mathcal{V}|=15,\ldots,20
): we increase the root boundary from
b_{0}=5
to
b_{0}=6
and continue increasing
r
. This changes the root-level factor in (
24
), produces a visible jump in
hybrid_root
, and increases the coherent EPR cost per invocation. By contrast, in this two-level benchmark
hybrid_all
effectively enumerates the full assignment space, so its query curve remains close to a straight line on the logarithmic scale.

Implication.

Measurement placement trades coherent depth against multiplicative replication of lower-level work. The simulations make this trade-off explicit at the circuit level: the query penalty is governed by the invocation recursion in (
23
), while the entanglement cost depends on where coherent interfaces remain. The main value of the study is therefore not a single universally best policy, but a concrete validation that Theorem
15
predicts how the preferred execution mode should depend on the available entanglement budget, allowable circuit depth, and latency constraints of the target network.
